3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 18.16267pt\raise-42.75447pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 18.35484pt\raise-42.5447pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 35.95248pt\raise-49.06262pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.73611pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{F}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.24518pt\raise-43.10431pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.087pt\raise-42.86783pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}}}\\
\Phi_{\langle a,b\rangle}^{0}(\bot_{\mathsk{pom}})&&\Phi_{\langle a,b\rangle}^{1}(\bot_{\mathsk{pom}})&&\Phi_{\langle a,b\rangle}^{2}(\bot_{\mathsk{pom}})&&\hskip-10.00002pt\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Phi_{\langle a,b\rangle}^{n}(\bot_{\mathsk{pom}})\end{array}

The supremum of this chain is clearly an infinite structure with a terminating branch for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and an infinitely ascending spine. The
\bot
node is pushed to a progressively higher level after each unrolling, so in the supremum it does not appear at all.

On the right of
Figure

2
, we show
\left\llbracket(\textsf{while}~b~\textsf{do}~a)\ ;\,a^{\prime}\right\rrbracket
, containing a loop with a single action in the body followed by another action.
As discussed in
Section

4.2
, sequentially composing another action
a^{\prime}
after a loop produces a copy of
a^{\prime}
for each branch, resulting in countably many copies of
a^{\prime}
. This approach is necessary to make
a^{\prime}
finitely preceded.

The semantics obeys a binary branching property, guaranteeing that test nodes branch into exactly two successors, one where the test passes and another where it fails. Further, variables can only be introduced into formulae after a test. From now on, we assume that all pomsets have this property, which is clearly preserved by the operations of
Section

4
.

Definition 13

(Binary Branching)
.

A pomset
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in{\mathsk{pom}}
has the
binary branching property
if, for every
\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and every
x\in N_{\alpha}
such that
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\in\mathsk{test}
,
\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)=\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
such that:

1.

\varphi_{\alpha}(y_{1})\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\land x
;

2.

\varphi_{\alpha}(y_{2})\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\land\lnot x
; and

3.

\mathsf{pred}_{\alpha}(y_{1})=\mathsf{pred}_{\alpha}(y_{2})=\{x\}
.

Furthermore, if
x
is not the successor of a test, then
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Leftrightarrow\bigwedge_{y\in\mathsf{pred}_{\alpha}(x)}\varphi_{\alpha}(y)
.

5.2
Linearization

We now discuss how to produce a state transformer from the pomset semantics of
Section

5.1
, which is useful for understanding the input-output behavior of programs resulting from scheduling concurrent threads.
This state transformer is obtained via
linearization
, which consists of considering all interleavings of the threads in order to interpret the semantics of a
complete
program as a function from inputs to collections of outputs. Once the program is linearized, more threads cannot be composed in parallel, as the causality information is gone.
Nevertheless, linearization is useful to understand the program’s behavior, and relate the behavior to other semantic models, as shown in
Sections

6
and
2
.

When performing linearization, we will interpret programs in a computational domain
D
, which must support nondeterminism—in order to model the different ways that threads can be interleaved—but may support additional computational effects too, which we will model using monads. Hence, we assume some familiarity with basic notions of monads and Kleisli categories; for a detailed introduction, we refer to
[
3
]
. More precisely, we need the following:

1.

A domain
D
and set of states
\mathcal{S}
, such that
\langle D(\mathcal{S}),\sqsubseteq\rangle
is a pointed
dcpo
with bottom
\bot_{D}
.

2.

An associative, commutative, and monotone nondeterminism operator
\mathord{\mathbin{\&}}\colon D(\mathcal{S})^{2}\to D(\mathcal{S})
.

3.

An additive monad in the category
dcpo

\langle D,\eta,(-)^{\dagger}\rangle
, meaning that:

(a)

the operations

\eta\colon X\to D(X)

and

(-)^{\dagger}\colon(X\to D(Y))\to D(X)\to D(Y)

obey the monad laws
f^{\dagger}\circ\eta=f
,
\eta^{\dagger}=\mathsf{id}
, and
(g^{\dagger}\circ f)^{\dagger}=g^{\dagger}\circ f^{\dagger}
.

(b)

Kleisli extension is Scott Continuous (
i.e.,

\sup_{f\in D}\sup_{d\in D^{\prime}}f^{\dagger}(d)=(\sup D)^{\dagger}(\sup D^{\prime})
),
strict (
i.e.,

f^{\dagger}(\bot_{D})=\bot_{D}
), and additive (
i.e.,

f^{\dagger}(d\mathbin{\&}d^{\prime})=f^{\dagger}(d)\mathbin{\&}f^{\dagger}(d^{\prime})
)
[
69
,
72
]
.

4.

An interpretation function for actions
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket{act}\colon\mathsk{act}\to\mathcal{S}\to D(\mathcal{S})
that is monotone (
i.e.,

\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket{act}\!(s)\sqsubseteq\left\llbracket a^{\prime}\right\rrbracket{act}\!(s)
if
a\leq{act}a^{\prime}
) and one for tests
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket{test}\colon\mathsk{test}\to\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{B}
.

There are many domains that obey these properties, depending on which
computational effects
are present. These include: the Hoare, Smyth, and Plotkin powerdomains for nondeterministic computation
[
47
,
55
,
1
]
(where the latter two additionally deal with nontermination), the convex powerset
[
43
,
20
]
and the powerdomain of indexed valuations
[
63
,
64
,
66
]
(for mixed probabilistic and nondeterministic computation), and other domains for nondeterminism with exceptions
[
69
,
72
]
. In
Theorems

15
and
16
, we will show that standard semantics in two of those domains are recovered from our pomset semantics.

\displaystyle\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi,S)

\displaystyle\triangleq\{x\in N_{\alpha}\setminus S\mid{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha}\subseteq S,\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\psi,S)(s)

\displaystyle\triangleq\eta(s)\quad\text{if}~\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi,S)=\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\psi,S)(s)

\displaystyle\triangleq\bignd_{x\in\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi,S)}\!\!\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}\left(\alpha,\psi,S\cup\{x\}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket{act}(s)\right)&\text{if}~\lambda_{\alpha}(x)=a\in\mathsk{act}\\
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\psi\land\llparenthesis x={\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket}{test}(s)\rrparenthesis,S\cup\{x\})(s)&\text{if}~\lambda_{\alpha}(x)=b\in\mathsk{test}\\
\bot_{D}&\text{if}~\lambda_{\alpha}(x)=\bot\\
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\psi,S\cup\{x\})(s)&\text{if}~\lambda_{\alpha}(x)=\mathsf{fork}\end{array}\right.

Figure 3:
Linearization for finite
lpof
s
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}\colon{\mathsk{lpof}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(\mathsk{label})\times\mathsk{form}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathsk{nodes})\to\mathcal{S}\to D(\mathcal{S})
.

We will start by defining a linearization operation on finite
lpof
s, which is shown in
Figure

3
.
Linearization is defined recursively, until there are no more nodes to schedule. It must be defined on finite structures, otherwise the recursion is not well-founded, but we can extend linearization to infinite structures using the extension lemma.
In
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\psi,S)
, the set
S\subseteq N_{\alpha}
contains all the nodes that have already been processed and
\psi
is a path condition, indicating the outcomes of the tests associated to the nodes in
S
.

The function
(\alpha,\psi,S)
gives the nodes that are ready to be scheduled, which includes all nodes that obey the path condition
\psi
, and whose predecessors are in
S
. If
(\alpha,\psi,S)
is empty, then
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\psi,S)
simply returns the current state
s
using the monad unit
\eta
. If not, then it nondeterministically selects a next node, where
\bignd_{i\in I}d_{i}\triangleq d_{i_{1}}\mathbin{\&}\cdots\mathbin{\&}d_{i_{n}}
, for any finite index set
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\}
. If the next node is an action, then it interprets the action using
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket{act}
, and then uses Kleisli composition to compose the result with the linearization of the remaining
lpof
. If the next node is a test, then that test is evaluated and the result is added to the path condition, where
\llparenthesis x=1\rrparenthesis\triangleq x
and
\llparenthesis x=0\rrparenthesis\triangleq\lnot x
. If the next node is
\bot
, then the linearization is
\bot_{D}
, and
\mathsf{fork}
is treated like a no-op.

We next use
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}
to define linearization on finite pomsets
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}\colon{\mathsk{pom}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(\mathsk{label})\to\mathcal{S}\to D(\mathcal{S})
as

\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}([\alpha])\triangleq\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\mathsf{true},\emptyset)
.
Clearly
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\mathsf{true},\emptyset)=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\beta,\mathsf{true},\emptyset)
if
\alpha\equiv\beta
, so
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
can be defined for any arbitrary representative
lpof

\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
. Finally, linearization is extended to infinite pomsets
\mathcal{L}\colon{\mathsk{pom}}\to\mathcal{S}\to D(\mathcal{S})
by taking the supremum over all of the finite approximations:

\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\triangleq\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
.
Linearization over finite pomsets is monotone (
Lemma

60
), therefore the extension is well-defined and Scott continuous.

To ensure that linearization acts as desired, we provide a sanity check lemma to relate the linearized semantics to well-known counterparts. Linearizing
skip
gives the monad unit; linearizing a singleton action has the same behavior as interpreting the action; linearizing a sequential composition is equal to Kleisli composition of the individual linearizations;
linearizing an if-statement is equal to linearizing one of the two branches, depending on the truth of the guard; and
the linearization of a while-loop is equal to the least fixed point over a different characteristic function
\Psi_{\langle f,b\rangle}
. The function
\Psi_{\langle f,b\rangle}
inherits Scott continuity from the Kleisli composition operator, therefore the fixed point exists.

Lemma 14

(Linearization)
.

The following properties hold:

\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket\textsf{skip}\right\rrbracket)=\eta\par\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket)=\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket{act}\par\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket C_{1};C_{2}\right\rrbracket)=\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket)^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket)\par\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket\textsf{if}~b~\textsf{then}~C_{1}~\textsf{else}~C_{2}\right\rrbracket)(s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket)(s)&\text{if}~\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket{test}(s)=1\\
\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket)(s)&\text{if}~\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket{test}(s)=0\end{array}\right.\par\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket\textsf{while}~b~\textsf{do}~C\right\rrbracket)=\mathsf{lfp}\left(\Psi_{\langle\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket),b\rangle}\right)~\text{where}~\Psi_{\langle f,b\rangle}(g)(s)\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}g^{\dagger}(f(s))&\text{if}~\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket{test}(s)=1\\
\eta(s)&\text{if}~\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket{test}(s)=0\end{array}\right.

Lemma

14
is quite significant; it implies that
\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket)
corresponds to standard monadic semantics in a variety of domains. In particular, it brings us to our first adequacy theorem, showing that our pomset model recovers standard probabilistic semantics
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{S})
(shown in
Figure

4
of
Section

A.2
)
[
20
,
71
,
37
]
based on the convex powerset for a parallel-free fragment of our programming language.

Theorem 15
.

For any program
C\in\mathsk{cmd}
without parallel composition:
\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket)=\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
C
. The cases follow immediately from
Lemma

14
.
∎

6
Pomset Languages and Purely Nondeterministic Concurrency

Powerdomains give semantics to programs that combine nondeterminism with looping or recursion
[
47
,
55
]
. In this section, we create a powerdomain instantiation of our semantics from
Section

5
and prove that the resulting model corresponds to well-known models of non-probabilistic concurrent programs
[
27
,
34
]
.
We work in the
Hoare
or
lower
powerdomain, named as such for its connection to partial correctness and Hoare Logic
[
21
,
16
]
. Indeed, the Hoare powerdomain identifies the terminating traces of a program, but not that the program
always
terminates. From a concurrency perspective, this corresponds to
safety
properties
[
33
]
. For simplicity, we presume that the order over actions
\leq{act}
in this section is flat.

Since typical pomsets do not contain formulae
[
17
,
49
,
18
]
, they cannot encode control flow branching introduced by if-statements and while-loops. As we already saw in
Section

2
, concurrent semantics typically use
pomset languages
—sets of pomsets—where each individual pomset contains only the actions that occur in a single branch of computation
[
27
,
34
]
.

We emulate pomset language semantics using pomsets with formulae. First, we define a label set
\mathsk{label}_{\mathsf{PL}}\triangleq\mathsk{act}_{\mathsf{PL}}\cup\mathsk{test}_{\mathsf{PL}}\cup\{\mathsf{fork},\bot\}
, whose components are as before, but now
\mathsk{test}_{\mathsf{PL}}\triangleq\emptyset
, and instead
\mathsk{act}_{\mathsf{PL}}\triangleq\mathsk{act}\cup\{\textsf{assume}~b\mid b\in\mathsk{test}\}
includes an action
\textsf{assume}~b
for every test
b\in\mathsk{test}
, which guarantees that a certain condition holds in the current branch.
Pomset languages
\mathsk{pomLang}\triangleq\mathcal{P}({\mathsk{pom}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(\mathsk{label}_{\mathsf{PL}}))
are sets of finite pomsets over
\mathsk{label}_{\mathsf{PL}}
.

The pomset language semantics
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}\colon\mathsk{cmd}\to\mathsk{pomLang}
, which is standard
[
27
,
34
]
, is given in
Figure

5
of
Section

A.3
. In this model,
each
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}
corresponds to a particular sequence of test resolutions. When an
assume
fails, the trace is eliminated. When a branch occurs,
i.e.,
in an if-statement or while-loop, the set of traces is duplicated to account for both the “true” and “false” branches, as we saw in
Figure

1
. Loops are interpreted as the (possibly infinite) union of all finite traces, so infinite pomsets are not needed.

The Hoare powerdomain consists of sets of states, ordered by subset inclusion
\langle\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S}),\subseteq\rangle
, which is known to be a pointed
dcpo
with supremum given by union
\cup
and
\emptyset
as bottom. Let
\mathbin{\&}\triangleq\cup
and define the monad operations as
\eta(s)\triangleq\{s\}
and
f^{\dagger}(S)\triangleq\bigcup_{s\in S}f(s)
, which are well known to be Scott continuous, strict, and additive
[
69
]
.
Accordingly, action evaluation

\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathsk{act}_{\mathsf{PL}}}\colon\mathsk{act}_{\mathsf{PL}}\to\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})

is given below, where
assume
actions result in
\{s\}
, if the test is true in state
s
, and
\emptyset
, otherwise.
We also define a specialized linearization operation
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{PL}}\colon\mathsk{pomLang}\to\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
, which is simply a union over the linearization of all traces.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsk{act}_{\mathsf{PL}}}\!(s)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket{act}(s)&\text{if}~a\in\mathsk{act}\\
\{s\}&\text{if}~a=\textsf{assume}~b,\ \left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket{test}\!(s)=1\\
\emptyset&\text{if}~a=\textsf{assume}~b,\ \left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket{test}\!(s)=0\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{PL}}(S)(s)

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in S}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})(s)

Since all formulae are
\mathsf{true}
, the next nodes to schedule are exactly those whose predecessors have all been processed,
i.e.,

(\alpha,\mathsf{true},S)=\{x\in N_{\alpha}\setminus S\mid{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha}\subseteq S\}
. This corresponds to standard interleaving definitions of linearization
[
49
]
.
We now prove that pomset language semantics
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}
(
Figure

5
) corresponds exactly to the Hoare powerdomain instance of our semantic model from
Section

5
. The proof relies on a translation
\mathsf{tr}\colon{\mathsk{pom}}\to\mathsk{pomLang}
, which recovers a pomset language from a pomset with formulae, and is defined in
Appendix

H
.

Theorem 16

(Equivalence of Semantics)
.

The following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{{\mathsk{cmd}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket}
\scriptstyle{\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathsk{pom}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{tr}}
\textstyle{{\mathsk{pomLang}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{PL}}}
\textstyle{{(\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S}))}}

The upper commuting triangle does not depend on the Hoare powerdomain, so it is tempting to say that pomset languages give an adequate model in other domains too. However, as we showed in
Section

2
, just because a semantic structure exists does not mean that it conveys the desired meaning.
Indeed, the fact that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{PL}}\circ\mathsf{tr}
relies on two particular properties of the Hoare powerdomain. First, if
h(x)=f(x)\mathbin{\&}g(x)
, then
h^{\dagger}(S)=f^{\dagger}(S)\mathbin{\&}g^{\dagger}(S)
, which is invalid in probabilistic domains. In fact, this corresponds to the problematic
Equation

3
from
Section

2
.
Second,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{PL}}
is computed as a union over a possibly infinite set (the union is infinite whenever the program contains a while-loop). Unbounded nondeterminism is known to cause problems in many domains—including the Smyth and Plotkin powerdomains
[
2
,
4
,
57
]
—thus no infinitary version of
\mathbin{\&}
exists.

So, although programs in any domain
can
be interpreted as pomset languages, those semantic objects may not give an adequate meaning to the program. Particularly in probabilistic domains—
i.e.,
the convex powerset—prior pomset language models
[
27
,
34
]
do not give us a way to find the relative probabilities of the different outcomes after actually running the program.
Our new approach, using a single structure that records both causality and control flow, is more suitable for interpreting the semantics of concurrent programs in new domains, such as probabilistic computation. As we saw in
Section

2
and
Theorem

15
, pomsets with formulae accurately capture the concurrent behavior of these programs, while also allowing us to recover the precise probabilities of each outcome.

7
Discussion and Related Work

We developed a new semantic structure—pomsets with formulae—and studied its domain-theoretic properties, which we used as basis to provide semantics for a probabilistic concurrent imperative language with unbounded loops. Our work builds on a rich history of using partial orders and pomsets in denotational models of concurrent programs
[
18
,
48
,
17
]
, which have been used as a semantic basis for concurrent separation logic
[
8
]
, concurrent Kleene algebras
[
22
,
27
,
34
,
26
]
, and other applications
[
29
,
14
,
25
]
.

While there are links between pomset semantics and operational models of concurrency
[
7
]
, pomsets are often preferred for their finer notion of concurrency compared to the interleaving semantics offered by operational models. This is especially useful in the context of weak memory
[
29
,
25
]
.
Probabilistic event structures model probabilistic process algebras
[
28
,
68
,
65
,
67
]
, whereas we model a full imperative language with sequential composition, control flow, and unbounded loops, for which adding formulae was essential.

Concurrency has previously been combined with other computational effects in limited ways. For example, semantics for probabilistic concurrent programs have been defined in both operational
[
59
,
58
]
and denotational
[
73
]
styles, but these approaches are limited to programs with bounded looping constructs.
The inclusion of unbounded looping adds significant complexity in the probabilistic case. Whereas finite-trace models are sufficient for many non-probabilistic scenarios, unbounded looping in probabilistic programs requires infinite traces, as the probability of termination may only become 1 in the limit. A proper theory of infinite traces requires pomsets to be enriched with a
dcpo

[
39
,
38
]
or metric
[
9
]
structure; prior work in this area was very informative to our own development, although the inclusion of deterministic branching in our own pomset structure added significant complexity.

An operational model of probabilistic concurrency has been developed based on Markov Decision Processes
[
15
]
. This semantics is used to lower bound expected values, but does not straightforwardly extend to more complex domains such as the convex powerset, which give the full set of distributions over outcomes.
In addition, a denotational model has been developed
[
45
]
, where the underlying semantic structure is obtained as the solution to a domain equation. Our construction is more concrete, giving a full account of the causality in the program and thus capturing non-interleaving models of concurrency too.

Branching pomsets were recently introduced to model choices in choreographic programs
[
14
,
12
,
13
]
. While branching pomsets can, in theory, contain infinitely many nodes, the domain theoretic properties needed to approximate infinite structures have not been explored, which was a significant focus of this paper. Indeed, our extension lemma (
Lemma

10
) was necessary for linearization, without which we would not have been able to relate our pomeset model to the known convex powerset semantics
[
20
,
37
,
71
]
.

Going forward, it would be interesting to explore the applicability of pomsets with formulae to convex powerdomain constructions other than Smyth
[
62
,
61
,
30
,
60
]
, or to other domains for mixing probabilities and nondeterminism including indexed valuations
[
64
,
66
,
63
]
and multisets of distributions
[
24
,
32
]
. While our linearization procedure is based on an
omniscient
scheduler, we are also interested in exploring more restricted models including oblivious schedulers (which cannot see the outcomes of random sampling), and fair schedulers. This will be challenging, as those models are non-compositional and therefore linearization on infinite pomsets could not be defined via
Lemma

10
. Still, pomsets with formulae are a good basis for studying these questions, as the structure itself makes no assumptions about how scheduling of parallel branches occurs.

Appendix A
Preliminaries and Omitted Definitions

A.1
Order Theory and Domain Theory

Definition 17

(Poset)
.

A partially ordered set, or poset, is a set equipped with a partial order: that is, a pair
\langle X,\leq\rangle
consisting of a set
X
and an order relation
\mathord{\leq}\subseteq X\times X
which is reflexive, transitive and antisymmetric. When the order is instead irreflexive (
i.e.,

x\not<x
for all
x\in X
), the poset is called strict and the order relation is denoted by
<
.

Given a strict poset
\langle X,<\rangle
and
x\in X
, we define the upward and downward closures:

\displaystyle{x\mathclose{\uparrow}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{y\in X\mid x<y\}

\displaystyle{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{y\in X\mid y<x\};

and its set of immediate successors and predecessors as

\mathsf{succ}(x)\triangleq\{y\in{x\mathclose{\uparrow}}\ \mid\ \not\exists z.(x<z<y)\}\qquad\qquad\mathsf{pred}(x)\triangleq\{y\in{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}\ \mid\ \not\exists z.(y<z<x)\}

All the above operations can easily be extended to sets of elements,
e.g.,

{X\mathclose{\uparrow}}\triangleq\bigcup_{x\in X}{x\mathclose{\uparrow}}
, for any set
X
.
The set of minimal and maximal nodes of a poset is given by:

\displaystyle\max\triangleq\{x\in X\mid\mathsf{succ}(x)=\emptyset\}\qquad\min\triangleq\{x\in X\mid\mathsf{pred}(x)=\emptyset\}

To define our new structures we need a few more definitions on posets that will play a role in proving the existence of fixed points, essential for giving a semantics to unbounded loops. First, we recall the notion of downward closed sets.

Definition 18

(Downward Closure)
.

Given a poset
\langle X,<\rangle
, a set
Y\subseteq X
is downward closed, written
Y\subseteq_{\downarrow}X
, iff for all
y\in Y
it holds that
{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}\,\subseteq Y
.

Second, we need a notion of a poset being finitely preceded.

Definition 19

(Finitely Preceded)
.

A poset
\langle X,<\rangle
is
finitely preceded
if there are finitely many elements smaller than each element; that is:
|{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}|<\infty
, for all
x\in X
.

Third, we define the level of elements in the poset.

Definition 20

(Level
[
38
]
)
.

Given a finitely preceded poset
\langle X,<\rangle
, define
\mathsf{lev}\colon X\to\mathbb{N}
:

\mathsf{lev}(x)\triangleq\sup\left\{n\ \middle|\!\!\begin{array}[]{l}\exists x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}\in X.\ x_{0}\in\min\ \wedge\ x_{n}=x\ \wedge\ \forall i<n.\ x_{i+1}\in\mathsf{succ}(x_{i})\end{array}\right\}

We also define its inverse
\mathsf{lev}^{-1}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{P}(X)
:

\mathsf{lev}^{-1}(n)\triangleq\{x\in X\mid\mathsf{lev}(x)=n\}

Definition 21
.

The satisfaction relation
\mathord{\vDash}\subseteq(\mathsk{nodes}\to\mathbb{B})\times\mathsk{form}
for Boolean formulae is given below:

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\vDash\mathsf{true}

always

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\vDash\mathsf{false}

never

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\vDash\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2}

\displaystyle\text{iff}\quad v\vDash\psi_{1}~\text{and}~v\vDash\psi_{2}

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\vDash\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2}

\displaystyle\text{iff}\quad v\vDash\psi_{1}~\text{or}~v\vDash\psi_{2}

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\vDash\lnot\psi

\displaystyle\text{iff}\quad v\not\vDash\psi

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\vDash x

\displaystyle\text{iff}\quad v(x)=1

A.2
The Convex Powerdomain

We begin by providing the omitted definitions from
Section

2
, regarding the
dcpo
structure of the convex powerset. We start by defining the order on probability distributions. For any set
X
, let
X_{\bot}=X\cup\{\bot\}
and the order over distributions
\mathord{\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(X_{\bot})\times\mathcal{D}(X_{\bot})
be defined as follows:

\mu\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\nu\quad\text{iff}\quad\forall x\in X.\ \mu(x)\leq\nu(x)

Note that this means that
\mu(\bot)\geq\nu(\bot)
if
\mu\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\nu
, and that the bottom of the order is
\bot_{\mathcal{D}}=[\bot\mapsto 1]
. A set of distributions
S\subseteq\mathcal{D}(X_{\bot})
is
upward closed
if, for all
\mu\in S
, it holds that
\mu\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\nu
implies
\nu\in S
. In addition,
S
is
Cauchy closed
if it is closed in the product of Euclidean topologies
[
37
, Definition 5.4.3]
. Now, the convex powerset is defined as follows:

\mathcal{C}(X)\triangleq\left\{S\subseteq\mathcal{D}(X_{\bot})\;\;\middle|\;\;\text{$S$ is nonempty, convex, upward closed, and Cauchy closed}\right\}

Convex powersets are ordered via the Smyth order
[
55
]

\mathord{\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(X)\times\mathcal{C}(X)
, defined below:

S\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}T\quad\text{iff}\quad\forall\nu\in T.\ \ \exists\mu\in S.\ \ \mu\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{D}}\nu

Since the sets above are upward closed, the Smyth order collapses to reverse subset inclusion
S\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}T
iff
S\supseteq T
. This makes the
convex powerdomain

\langle\mathcal{C}(X),\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}\rangle
a pointed
dcpo
with suprema given by set intersection and bottom
\bot_{\mathcal{C}}=\mathcal{D}(X_{\bot})
being the set of all distributions, for the full proof refer to
[
71
]
.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\textsf{skip}\right\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}\!(s)

\displaystyle\triangleq\eta(s)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket C_{1};C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}\!(s)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}^{\dagger}\left(\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}(s)\right)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\textsf{if}~b~\textsf{then}~C_{1}~\textsf{else}~C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}\!(s)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}\!(s)&\text{if}~\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket{test}(s)=1\\
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}\!(s)&\text{if}~\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket{test}(s)=0\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\textsf{while}~b~\textsf{do}~C\right\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}\!(s)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{lfp}(\Psi_{\langle\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}},b\rangle})(s)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}\!(s)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket{act}(s)

Figure 4:
Convex powerset semantics
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\mathsk{cmd}\to\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{S})
due to
[
20
,
71
,
37
]
,
where
\Psi
is defined in
Lemma

14
.

Finally, we give the definitions for the monad operations
[
23
]
: unit
\eta\colon X\to\mathcal{C}(X)
and Kleisli extension
(-)^{\dagger}\colon(X\to\mathcal{C}(Y))\to\mathcal{C}(X)\to\mathcal{C}(Y)
.

\displaystyle\eta(x)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left[x\mapsto 1\right]

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}(S)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu)}\mu(x)\cdot\nu_{x}\;\;\middle|\;\;\mu\in S,\forall x\in\mathsf{supp}(\mu).\ \nu_{x}\in f_{\bot}(x)\right\}

where
(-)_{\bot}\colon(X\to\mathcal{C}(Y))\to X_{\bot}\to\mathcal{C}(Y)
is defined as follows:

f_{\bot}(x)\triangleq f(x)
if
x\in X
and
f_{\bot}(\bot)\triangleq\bot_{\mathcal{C}}
otherwise.

As is required in
Section

5.2
, it is proven in
[
71
]
that the Kleisli extension for
\mathcal{C}
defined above is Scott continuous and additive:

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in D,x\in D^{\prime}}f^{\dagger}(S)

\displaystyle=(\sup D)^{\dagger}(\sup D^{\prime})

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}(S\mathbin{\&}T)

\displaystyle=f^{\dagger}(S)\mathbin{\&}f^{\dagger}(T)

Based on these operations,
Figure

4
shows the convex powerset semantics for the parallel-free fragment of the language in (
1
),
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\mathsk{cmd}\to\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{S})
. This is precisely the same semantics used in prior work for reasoning about programs that are both nondeterministic and probabilistic
[
20
,
37
,
71
]
. This semantics does not capture parallel computation, which requires the more sophisticated semantic domains that we develop in this paper.
The Scott continuity property above ensures that
\Psi_{\langle\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathcal{C}},b\rangle}\colon(\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{S}))\to\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{S})
is Scott continuous, and therefore the semantics of loops is well defined.

A.3
Pomset Languages

As we saw in
Section

6
, a pomset language is a set of finite pomsets without any tests or formulae. Control flow is determined by
assume
actions, which keep or eliminate traces depending on the whether the corresponding test passes or fails.

\mathsk{pomLang}\triangleq\mathcal{P}({\mathsk{pom}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(\mathsk{label}_{\mathsf{PL}}))

We give the pomset language semantics for a nondeterministic language in
Figure

5
.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\textsf{skip}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathsf{fork}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{\boldsymbol{\langle}a\boldsymbol{\rangle}\}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket C_{1};C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}\fatsemi\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket C_{1}\,|\,C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}\parallel\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\textsf{if}~b~\textsf{then}~C_{1}~\textsf{else}~C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left(\left\{\boldsymbol{\langle}\textsf{assume}~b\boldsymbol{\rangle}\right\}\fatsemi\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}\right)\cup\left(\left\{\boldsymbol{\langle}\textsf{assume}~\lnot b\boldsymbol{\rangle}\right\}\fatsemi\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}\right)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\textsf{while}~b~\textsf{do}~C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{lfp}\left(\Xi_{\langle C,b\rangle}\right)

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad\Xi_{\langle C,b\rangle}(S)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left(\left\{\boldsymbol{\langle}\textsf{assume}~b\boldsymbol{\rangle}\right\}\fatsemi\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}\fatsemi S\right)\cup\left(\{\boldsymbol{\langle}\textsf{assume}~\lnot b\boldsymbol{\rangle}\}\fatsemi\left\llbracket\textsf{skip}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}\right)

\displaystyle S\fatsemi T

\displaystyle\triangleq\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in S,\boldsymbol{\beta}\in T\}

\displaystyle S\parallel T

\displaystyle\triangleq\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\parallel\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in S,\boldsymbol{\beta}\in T\}

Figure 5:
Standard pomset language semantics
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{PL}}\colon\mathsk{cmd}\to\mathsk{pomLang}
.

Appendix B
Operations on Pomset with Formulae: Examples

\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha_{1}}=y_{1}\lor\lnot y_{1}\Leftrightarrow\mathsf{true}&\Leftarrow&\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha_{2}}=y_{1}&\Leftarrow&\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha_{3}}=\mathsf{false}\\
\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha_{1}}=\emptyset&\subseteq&\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha_{2}}=N_{\alpha_{2}}\setminus\{z_{1}\}&\subseteq&\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha_{3}}=N_{\alpha_{3}}\\
\mathsf{br}_{\alpha_{1}}=\emptyset&\subseteq&\mathsf{br}_{\alpha_{2}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccr}\lnot y_{1}&\land&y_{2},\\
\lnot y_{1}&\land&\lnot y_{2}\end{array}\right\}&\subseteq&\mathsf{br}_{\alpha_{3}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcr}y_{1}&\land&y_{2},\\
y_{1}&\land&\lnot y_{2},\\
\lnot y_{1}&\land&y_{2},\\
\lnot y_{1}&\land&\lnot y_{2}\end{array}\right\}\end{array}

Figure 6:
An example of sequential composition and some of the functions used to define
\fatsemi
.

In
Figure

6
we give three
lpof
s and, for each of them, we provide the stuck nodes, the extensible ones, the branches, and the result of sequentially composing them with the singleton
lpof

w
.
To lighten notation, we assume that
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
and
\alpha_{3}
have the same nodes and we only show the
\bot
labels (associated to
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
in
\alpha_{1}
and just to
z_{1}
in
\alpha_{2}
).
In
\alpha_{3}
, there are no stuck nodes, since all maximal elements are non-
\bot
; hence, all nodes are extensible and the branches include all the possible Boolean combinations of
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
(this requires 4 copies of
w
when building
\alpha_{3}\fatsemi w
).
In
\alpha_{2}
the only stuck node is
z_{1}
, arising when
y_{1}
is true; all other nodes are extensible but the only branches to be considered are those in which
y_{1}
is not true (thus, we only need two copies of
w
here).
Finally, in
\alpha_{1}
all nodes are stuck, since the leftmost branch will always lead to
\bot
and so the whole
lpof
cannot be sequentially composed with anything else; for this reason,
\alpha_{1}
has no extensible node and no branch (thus,
\alpha_{1}\fatsemi w=\alpha_{1}
).

B.2
Unrolling Loops

In this section, we discuss the rationale for the copying behavior of sequential composition by demonstrating the semantic structures that are generated as the result of looping programs. We start with a simple program, which is a loop containing a single action as the body:

\textsf{while}~b~\textsf{do}~a

As defined in
Figure

2
, the semantics of this program is given by the following fixed point:

\left\llbracket\textsf{while}~b~\textsf{do}~a\right\rrbracket=\mathsf{lfp}\left(\Phi_{\langle a,b\rangle}\right)\qquad\text{where}\qquad\Phi_{\langle a,b\rangle}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\mathsf{guard}(b,\boldsymbol{\langle}a\boldsymbol{\rangle}\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\alpha},\left\llbracket\textsf{skip}\right\rrbracket)

By the Kleene fixed point theorem, the least fixed point above is given by the supremum over all the finite iterations of
\Phi_{\langle a,b\rangle}
applied to
\bot_{\mathsk{pom}}
. Unrolling this definition several times, we get the chains shown in
Section

5.1
.
Now suppose that we would like to sequentially compose another action after the loops, to obtain the program:

(\textsf{while}~b~\textsf{do}~a)\;\;;\;\;a^{\prime}
.
According to the definition of sequential composition from
Section

4.2
, the semantics of this program is obtained by composing a copy of
a^{\prime}
after each branch of
\left\llbracket\textsf{while}~b~\textsf{do}~a\right\rrbracket
. As we just saw, the branches of
\left\llbracket\textsf{while}~b~\textsf{do}~a\right\rrbracket
correspond to all of the terminating traces of the loop, and therefore we make countably many copies of
a^{\prime}
, as shown in the first option below.
We investigated the possibility of defining sequential composition without such a copying, similar to what was done for finite structures in
[
73
]
. However, this approach does not translate to the infinite case. To see why not, consider the second option below structure, obtained by composing
a^{\prime}
after
\textsf{while}~b~\textsf{do}~a
, without copying.

\left\llbracket(\textsf{while}~b~\textsf{do}~a)\;\;;\;\;a^{\prime}\right\rrbracket\;\;=\;\;\left(\vbox{\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern The node labelled as
a^{\prime}
is not finitely preceded; it has countably many predecessors, one for each terminating branch of the loop. Relaxing the finitely preceded requirement for
lpof
s is not an option; it breaks many of the proofs from
Appendix

E
, preventing us from approximating a pomset using finite structures and invalidating the extension lemma. Without the extension lemma, it is unclear how linearization could be defined, as well-founded recursion relies on the structure being finite.
Scott continuity of sequential composition comes into question as well. Scott continuity of
\fatsemi
means that the following equation holds:

\left(\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Phi^{n}_{\langle a,b\rangle}(\bot_{\mathsk{pom}})\right)\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\langle}a^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsf{?}}}{{=}}\qquad\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\Phi^{n}_{\langle a,b\rangle}(\bot_{\mathsk{pom}})\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\langle}a^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\right)

We investigated two possible expansions for the latter expression. The first possibility retained the requirement that nothing can proceed
\bot
; it is shown as the sequence below, ignoring the grey dotted arrows. The second possibility allowed
\bot
nodes to have successors, and is shown below, where the grey dotted arrows indicate standard causality.

0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-94.39769pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 7.53185pt\raise-94.39769pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 22.21649pt\raise-94.39769pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 40.15407pt\raise-94.39769pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{b\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.32816pt\raise-92.52982pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.73611pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.78035pt\raise-84.8408pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.93852pt\raise-84.60432pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.95192pt\raise-92.52982pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.73611pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{F}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.24461pt\raise-86.5715pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.08644pt\raise-86.33502pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}}}&\not\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{pom}}\cdots\\
\Phi_{\langle a,b\rangle}^{0}(\bot_{\mathsk{pom}})\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\langle}a^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}&&\Phi_{\langle a,b\rangle}^{1}(\bot_{\mathsk{pom}})\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\langle}a^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}&&\Phi_{\langle a,b\rangle}^{2}(\bot_{\mathsk{pom}})\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\langle}a^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}&&\Phi_{\langle a,b\rangle}^{3}(\bot_{\mathsk{pom}})\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\langle}a^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\end{array}

The causality from
\bot
to
a^{\prime}
is problematic, since
\bot
could be expanded into an infinite structure, again causing
a^{\prime}
to not be finitely preceded. But the lack of causality from
\bot
to
a^{\prime}
is also problematic, as in the next approximation,
\bot
will be replaced by the test
b
, which is supposed to be scheduled before
a^{\prime}
. This complicates monotonicity of linearization, as the allowable interleavings can change as the structure grows.

In any case, the sequence above does not form a chain—at least, not according to our current definition of
\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}
. The reason why this is not a chain is because the node labelled
a^{\prime}
gains more predecessors with each successive structure. We investigated several alternative definitions of
\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}
, which did allow for this sort of expansion, however each one had significant problems—
e.g.,
some were not transitive, and thus not partial orders; some were not
dcpo
s; and some caused
\Phi_{\langle C,b\rangle}
not to be monotone, meaning that loops could not be interpreted as fixed points using the Kleene fixed point theorem or the Knaster-Tarski theorem. In the course of our investigation, we found that the copying of structures in sequential composition was the best way to obtain a sound structure, obeying all the necessary laws.

Interestingly, this copying behavior corresponds to the following equational law of Probabilistic Guarded Kleene Algebra with Tests
[
52
]
, where sequencing a command
C
after a guarded choice is equivalent to including
C
in both branches:

(\textsf{if}~b~\textsf{then}~C_{1}~\textsf{else}~C_{2})\;\;;\;\;C\quad\equiv\quad\textsf{if}~b~\textsf{then}~C_{1};C~\textsf{else}~C_{2};C

Appendix C
Pomsets with Tests

C.1
Labelled Partial Orders

Let
\mathsf{nBot}_{\alpha}
denote the set of nodes not labelled with
\bot
,
i.e.,

\mathsf{nBot}_{\alpha}\triangleq\{x\in N_{\alpha}\mid\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\neq\bot\}
.

Lemma 22
.

Let
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
; then,
\mathsf{nBot}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathsf{nBot}_{\beta}
.

Proof.

By Def.
2
(
Item

1
and
3a
), we have that
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\leq\lambda_{\beta}(x)
, for all
x\in N_{\alpha}\subseteq N_{\beta}
.
Hence, if
x\in\mathsf{nBot}_{\alpha}
, then
\bot<\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\leq\lambda_{\beta}(x)
and so
x\in\mathsf{nBot}_{\beta}
.
∎

Lemma 23
.

If
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
, then
N_{\beta}\setminus N_{\alpha}={\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}
.

Proof.

We show the set inclusion in both ways.

First, take any
x\in N_{\beta}\setminus N_{\alpha}
. Since
x
is finitely proceeded, there must be a positive integer
n
and nodes
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in N_{\beta}
such that
x_{1}
is the root of
\beta
,
x_{n}=x
, and
x_{i+1}\in\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x_{i})
for all
1\leq i<n
. Since
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
, then
x_{1}
must also be the root of
\alpha
. Now, let
i
be smallest index such that
x_{i}\in N_{\alpha}
and
x_{i+1}\not\in N_{\alpha}
(note that
1<i<n
since
x_{1}\in N_{\alpha}
but
x=x_{n}\notin N_{\alpha}
). From
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
, we know that
\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x_{i})=\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x_{i})\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}
; so,
x_{i+1}\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}
that allows us to conclude
x\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}
, being
x_{i+1}<_{\beta}x_{n}=x
.

Now, take
x\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}
, i.e. there exists
z\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
such that
z<_{\beta}x
. If
x\in N_{\alpha}
, being
<_{\alpha}\ =\ <_{\beta}\cap\ (N_{\alpha}\times N_{\alpha})
, we would have that
z<_{\alpha}x
, in contradiction with
z\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
.
∎

Lemma 24
.

\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}
is a partial order.

Proof.

Reflexivity is trivial.
For antisymmetry, we have that
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
and
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
imply that

N_{\alpha}=N_{\beta}
; hence:

\mathord{<_{\alpha}}=\mathord{<_{\beta}}\cap(N_{\alpha}\times N_{\alpha})=\mathord{<_{\beta}}\cap(N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})=\mathord{<_{\beta}}

Then, let
x\in N_{\alpha}(=N_{\beta})
. Trivially,
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)=\varphi_{\beta}(x)
; furthermore,
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\leq\lambda_{\beta}(x)
and
\lambda_{\beta}(x)\leq\lambda_{\alpha}(x)
, so,
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)=\lambda_{\beta}(x)
, by antisimmetry of
\leq
.

For transitivity, let us assume that
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
and
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
, and prove that
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
. By hypothesis,
N_{\alpha}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\beta}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\gamma}
, so
N_{\alpha}\subseteq N_{\gamma}
; we have to prove that
N_{\alpha}
is downwards closed (w.r.t. to
\gamma
).
Take any
x\in N_{\alpha}
and
y<_{\gamma}x
. Since
N_{\alpha}\subseteq N_{\beta}
,
x\in N_{\beta}
and, being
N_{\beta}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\gamma}
, then
y\in N_{\beta}
. Further, since
\mathord{<_{\beta}}=\mathord{<_{\gamma}}\cap(N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})
, then
y<_{\beta}x
. Therefore
y\in N_{\alpha}
, since
N_{\alpha}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\beta}
.

For the second item of Definition
2
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}<_{\alpha}&=&<_{\beta}\cap\ (N_{\alpha}\times N_{\alpha})\\
&=&(<_{\gamma}\cap\ (N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta}))\cap(N_{\alpha}\times N_{\alpha})\\
&=&<_{\gamma}\cap\ ((N_{\beta}\cap N_{\alpha})\times(N_{\beta}\cap N_{\alpha}))\\
&=&<_{\gamma}\cap\ (N_{\alpha}\times N_{\alpha})\end{array}

Finally, let
x\in N_{\alpha}
. Trivially,
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)=\varphi_{\beta}(x)=\varphi_{\gamma}(x)
and
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\leq\lambda_{\beta}(x)\leq\lambda_{\gamma}(x)
; so,
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\leq\lambda_{\gamma}(x)
, by transitivity of
\leq
.
Moreover, we have:

\displaystyle\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathsf{succ}_{\gamma}(x)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\gamma}\right)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{succ}_{\gamma}(x)\setminus\left({\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\gamma}\cup{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{succ}_{\gamma}(x)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\gamma}

where the last equality holds thanks to
Lemma

23
: indeed,
{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}=N_{\beta}\setminus N_{\alpha}
,
{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\gamma}=N_{\gamma}\setminus N_{\beta}
and

{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\gamma}=N_{\gamma}\setminus N_{\alpha}
; we conclude since
(N_{\gamma}\setminus N_{\beta})\cup(N_{\beta}\setminus N_{\alpha})=(N_{\gamma}\setminus N_{\alpha})
.
∎

Lemma 25
.

Let
N_{\beta}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\alpha}
and

<_{\beta}\ =\ <_{\alpha}\cap\ (N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})
. Then:

1.

if
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)
and
x<_{\beta}y
, then
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)
;

2.

if
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)
, then
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)
;

3.

\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)\subseteq\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha}
, for every
x\in N_{\beta}
.

Proof.

For the first claim, if it was
x<_{\beta}z<_{\beta}y
, then
x<_{\alpha}z<_{\alpha}y
(being
<_{\beta}\ \subseteq\ <_{\alpha}
), in contradiction with
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)
.

For the second claim,
x<_{\beta}y
and
\mathord{<_{\beta}}\subseteq\mathord{<_{\alpha}}
imply that
x<_{\alpha}y
. Now, by contradiction, assume that
x<_{\alpha}z<_{\alpha}y
. Since
y\in N_{\beta}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\alpha}
, then
z\in N_{\beta}
; hence, being
<_{\beta}\ =\ <_{\alpha}\cap\ (N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})
, we would have that
x<_{\beta}z<_{\beta}y
, in contradiction with
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)
. So,
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)
.

For the third claim, let
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)
; by the second claim,
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)
.
If it was
y\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha}
, then there would exist
z\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}
such that
z<_{\alpha}y
; being
<_{\beta}\ =\ <_{\alpha}\cap\ (N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})
, we would have that
z<_{\beta}y
, i.e.
z\not\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}
.
∎

Lemma 26
.

If
D\subseteq{\mathsk{lpof}}(L)
is a directed set, then
{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta}={x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta^{\prime}}
for any
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in D
such that
x\in N_{\beta}\cap N_{\beta^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Take any
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in D
such that
x\in N_{\beta}
and
x\in N_{\beta^{\prime}}
.
Since
D
is directed, there must be a
\gamma\in D
such that
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
and
\beta^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
.
Now, take any
y<_{\beta}x
, clearly this means that
y<_{\gamma}x
, and since
N_{\beta^{\prime}}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\gamma}
, then
y\in N_{\beta^{\prime}}
, and so
y\in{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta^{\prime}}
, so we have showed that
{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta}\subseteq{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta^{\prime}}
. By an equivalent argument, we get that
{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta^{\prime}}\subseteq{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta}
, therefore
{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta}={x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta^{\prime}}
.
∎

Lemma 27
.

For any directed set
D\subseteq{\mathsk{lpof}}(L)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a finite set
X
such that
\{x\mid\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(x)=n\}\subseteq X
for all
\beta\in D
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
n
. Let
n=1
, take any
\beta\in D
, and let
x
be the root of
\beta
. Since
D
is directed, then
x
is the root of every
\beta^{\prime}\in D
, therefore letting
X=\{x\}
, we have that
\{y\mid\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(y)=1\}=X
for all
\beta\in D
.

Now, suppose that the claim holds for all
k<n
, and let
X_{k}
be the finite set such that
\{x\mid\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(x)=k\}\subseteq X_{k}
for each
k<n
. Also, let
X=\bigcup_{k<n}X_{k}
, so
\{x\mid\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(x)<n\}\subseteq X
, which is also finite. This means that only finitely many
\bot
nodes can appear before level
n
. Now construct
A
as follows: for each
x\in X
, select a
\beta\in D
such that
\lambda_{\beta}(x)\neq\bot
, if such a
\beta
exists. So
|A|\leq|X|
, which is clearly finite. Since
A
is a finite subset of
D
, which is directed, then there must be some
\gamma\in D
that is an upper bound of all the elements in
A
. This means that for all
x\in X
,
x\notin\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}
for some
\beta\in D
iff
x\notin\mathsf{Bot}_{\gamma}
.

Now let
Y=\{x\mid\mathsf{lev}_{\gamma}(x)=n\}
. Take any
\beta\in D
, so there is a
\beta^{\prime}\in D
that is an upper bound of
\beta
and
\gamma
. By construction,
\beta^{\prime}
can only extend
\gamma
after level
n
, and so the elements of
\gamma
at level
n
must be the same as the elements of
\beta^{\prime}
and since
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta^{\prime}
, then
\{x\mid\mathsf{lev}_{\beta^{\prime}}(x)=n\}\subseteq Y
.
∎

See
6

Proof.

We need to show that:

1.

\alpha\in{\mathsk{lpof}}(L)
;

2.

\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
, for all
\beta\in D
; and

3.

\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
, for all
\gamma
s.t.
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
(for all
\beta\in D
).

We start with property (
1
). First, clearly
\alpha
is single-rooted, since
D
is a directed set and therefore every
\beta\in D
must have the same root. We now show that every element of
N_{\alpha}
is finitely proceeded.
Take any
x\in N_{\alpha}
, so
x\in N_{\beta}
for some
\beta\in D
.
By
Lemma

26
, we know that
{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta}={x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta^{\prime}}
for all
\beta^{\prime}\in D
, therefore
{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\beta^{\prime}\in A}{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta^{\prime}}={x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta}
, which much be finite.

Next, we show that finitely many elements appear at level
n
. By
Lemma

27
, we know that there is a finite set
X
such that
\{x\mid\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(x)=n\}\subseteq X
for all
\beta\in D
. Following from
Lemma

26
,
\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(x)=\mathsf{lev}_{\beta^{\prime}}(x)=\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)
for all
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in D
and
x\in N_{\alpha}
, so the elements at level
n
in
\alpha
are the elements at level
n
from all
\beta\in D
. This gives us:

\{x\mid\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)=n\}=\bigcup_{\beta\in D}\{x\mid\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(x)=n\}\subseteq X

Now, take any
x\in N_{\alpha}
. If
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)=\bot
, then
\lambda_{\beta}(x)=\bot
for all
\beta\in D
such that
x\in N_{\beta}
. This means that
x
has no successors in any
\beta\in D
, therefore it has no successors in
\alpha
.

If
x<_{\alpha}y
, then
x<_{\beta}y
for some
\beta\in D
. Since
D
is directed,
\varphi_{\beta}(x)=\varphi_{\beta^{\prime}}(x)
for all
\beta^{\prime}\in D
that contain
x
(and similarly for
y
); therefore,
\varphi_{\alpha}(y)=\varphi_{\beta}(y)\Rightarrow\varphi_{\beta}(x)=\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
. Finally, take any
x\in N_{\alpha}
, so
x\in N_{\beta}
for some
\beta\in D
. By construction,
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)=\varphi_{\beta}(x)=\psi_{x}
and, by
Lemma

26
,
{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha}={x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta}
; so, since
\mathsf{vars}(\varphi_{\beta}(x))\subseteq{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta}
, then
\mathsf{vars}(\varphi_{\alpha}(x))\subseteq{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha}
.

We next prove property (
2
): we choose any
\beta\in D
and show all conditions of
\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}
.

First, we prove that
N_{\beta}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\alpha}
; the inclusion is by construction. Then, take
x\in N_{\beta}
and
y<_{\alpha}x
; by construction, there must exist a
\beta^{\prime}\in D
such that
y<_{\beta^{\prime}}x
. Take
\gamma\in D
to be an upper bound of
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
, which must exist since
D
is a directed set. So
\beta^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
and therefore
y<_{\gamma}x
. Since
N_{\beta}
is a downward closed subset of
N_{\gamma}
(being
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
), it must be that
y\in N_{\beta}
.

For the order, we proceed as follows:

\displaystyle\mathord{<_{\alpha}}\cap(N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})

\displaystyle=\left(\bigcup_{\beta^{\prime}\in D}\mathord{<_{\beta^{\prime}}}\right)\cap\left(N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\beta^{\prime}\in D}\left(\mathord{<_{\beta^{\prime}}}\cap\left(N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ddagger}}{{=}}\mathord{<_{\beta}}

For the step (
\ddagger
), we prove that, for every
\beta^{\prime}\in D
and every
(x,y)\in\ <_{\beta^{\prime}}\ \cap\ (N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})
, we have that
(x,y)\in\ <_{\beta}
. Let
\gamma
be the upper bound of
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
; then,
x<_{\gamma}y
(being
x<_{\beta^{\prime}}y
and
\beta^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
). Since
<_{\beta}\ =\mathord{<_{\gamma}}\cap(N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})
(because
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
), we conclude
x<_{\beta}y
.

Now, fix
x\in N_{\beta}
; since
D
is directed,
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)=\psi_{x}=\varphi_{\beta}(x)
and, by definition of sup,
\lambda_{\beta}(x)\leq\lambda_{\alpha}(x)
.
Finally, by
Lemma

25
(3),
\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)\subseteq\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha}
. We now show the reverse inclusion. Suppose that
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)
and
y\notin{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha}
; by construction, there must be some
\beta^{\prime}\in D
such that
x<_{\beta^{\prime}}y
. By
Lemma

25
(1),
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\beta^{\prime}}(x)
.
Since
D
is directed, there is a
\gamma\in D
that is an upper bound of
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
.
Since
\beta^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
, then
\mathsf{succ}_{\beta^{\prime}}(x)=\mathsf{succ}_{\gamma}(x)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta^{\prime}}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\gamma}
; so,
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\gamma}(x)
. Since
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
, then we also have
\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)=\mathsf{succ}_{\gamma}(x)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\gamma}
. Since
y\notin{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha}
, then
y
also cannot be in
{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\gamma}
since
\mathord{<_{\gamma}}\subseteq\mathord{<_{\alpha}}
by construction; therefore, it must be that
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)
.

Let us now move to property (
3
): we fix
\gamma
satisfying
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
(for all
\beta\in D
) and show that
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
.

We know that
N_{\beta}\subseteq N_{\gamma}
for all
\beta\in D
, therefore
N_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\beta\in D}N_{\beta}\subseteq N_{\gamma}
. We now need to show that
N_{\alpha}
is downwards closed. Take any
x\in N_{\alpha}
and
y<_{\gamma}x
. Since
x\in N_{\alpha}
, then
x\in N_{\beta}
for some
\beta\in D
. Since
N_{\beta}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\gamma}
, then
y\in N_{\beta}
, and therefore
y\in N_{\alpha}
.

Second,

\displaystyle\mathord{<_{\gamma}}\cap(N_{\alpha}\times N_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\mathord{<_{\gamma}}\cap\left(\bigcup_{\beta\in D}N_{\beta}\times\bigcup_{\beta\in D}N_{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\dagger}}{{=}}\mathord{<_{\gamma}}\cap\left(\bigcup_{\beta\in D}(N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})\right)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\beta\in D}(\mathord{<_{\gamma}}\cap(N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta}))

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\beta\in D}\mathord{<_{\beta}}\ =\ \mathord{<_{\alpha}}

where the step marked with (
\dagger
) follows using two set inclusions. The first is
\bigcup_{\beta\in D}(N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})\subseteq\bigcup_{\beta\in D}N_{\beta}\times\bigcup_{\beta\in D}N_{\beta}
, which is immediate by the properties of cartesian product. For the reverse inclusion, take any
(x,y)\in\bigcup_{\beta\in D}N_{\beta}\times\bigcup_{\beta\in D}N_{\beta}
, which means that there exist
\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}\in D
such that
x\in N_{\beta^{\prime}}
and
y\in N_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}
.
Since
D
is a directed set, there exists
\hat{\beta}\in D
that is an upper bound of
\beta^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime\prime}
; thus,
N_{\beta^{\prime}}\subseteq N_{\hat{\beta}}
and
N_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}\subseteq N_{\hat{\beta}}
and so

N_{\beta^{\prime}}\times N_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}\subseteq N_{\hat{\beta}}\times N_{\hat{\beta}}
.
The latter implies that
(x,y)\in N_{\hat{\beta}}\times N_{\hat{\beta}}\subseteq\bigcup_{\beta\in D}(N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})
, as desired.

To conclude, take any
x\in N_{\alpha}
; since
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
(for all
\beta
), we know that
\varphi_{\beta}(x)=\varphi_{\gamma}(x)
and
\lambda_{\beta}(x)\leq\lambda_{\gamma}(x)
, for all
\beta
that contain
x
.
This allows us to obtain that
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)=\varphi_{\gamma}(x)
and
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\leq\lambda_{\gamma}(x)
.

Finally, by
Lemma

25
(3) (with
\alpha
in place of
\beta
and
\gamma
in place of
\alpha
therein),
\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)\subseteq\mathsf{succ}_{\gamma}(x)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\gamma}
. We now show the reverse inclusion too.
Let
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\gamma}(x)
and
y\notin{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\gamma}
. Since
y
is finitely proceeded, we know that
{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\gamma}
is finite, therefore
{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\gamma}\cap N_{\alpha}
is finite and
\lambda_{\alpha}(z)\neq\bot
for each
z\in{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\gamma}\cap N_{\alpha}
since
y\notin{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\gamma}
. We now construct a set
A
as follows: for each
z\in{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\gamma}\cap N_{\alpha}
, select a
\beta_{z}\in D
such that
z\in N_{\beta_{z}}
and
\lambda_{\beta_{z}}(z)\neq\bot
, which must exist since
\lambda_{\alpha}(z)\neq\bot
. Since
A
is a finite subset of
D
, which is directed, there is an element
\beta\in D
which is an upper bound of all the elements in
A
; therefore,
{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\gamma}\cap N_{\alpha}\subseteq N_{\beta}
and
\lambda_{\beta}(z)\neq\bot
for each
z\in{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\gamma}\cap N_{\alpha}
.
Since
\beta\in D
, we also know that
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
and, since
x<_{\gamma}y
and
x\in N_{\alpha}
, then
x\in N_{\beta}
; hence,
\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)=\mathsf{succ}_{\gamma}(x)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\gamma}
, so either
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)
or
y\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\gamma}
. In the first case, we are done since
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
and therefore
\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)\subseteq\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)
by
Lemma

25
(3). The second case is a contradiction, since it implies that there is some
z\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}
such that
z<_{\gamma}y
; hence,
z\in{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\gamma}\cap N_{\beta}\subseteq{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\gamma}\cap N_{\alpha}
, where the last inclusion holds since
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
. This implies that
\lambda_{\alpha}(z)\neq\bot
, i.e.
z\not\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}
.
∎

Corollary 28
.

\langle{\mathsk{lpof}}(L),\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\rangle
is a
dcpo
.

Proof.

Follows immediately from
Lemmas

24
and
6
.
∎

C.2
Truncation of Lpos

First, we define some notation for lpos, similar to that of pomsets:

{\mathsk{lpof}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L)\triangleq\{\alpha\in{\mathsk{lpof}}(L)\mid|N_{\alpha}|<\infty\}\qquad\qquad{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}\triangleq\{\beta\in{\mathsk{lpof}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L)\mid\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha\}

Definition 29

(Truncation)
.

Let
\alpha\in{\mathsk{lpof}}(L)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}=\langle N,<,\lambda,\varphi\rangle
where

•

N\triangleq\{x\in N_{\alpha}\mid\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)\leq n\}
;

•

<\ \triangleq\ <_{\alpha}\cap\ (N\times N)
;

•

for all
x\in N
, let
\lambda(x)\triangleq\lambda_{\alpha}(x)
, if
\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)<n
, and
\lambda(x)\triangleq\bot
, otherwise;

•

for all
x\in N
, let
\varphi(x)\triangleq\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
.

Essentially, the truncation of
\alpha
with
n
consists in keeping
\alpha
unchanged for all nodes
x\in N_{\alpha}
such that
\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)<n
and changing the labels of all nodes
x\in\mathsf{lev}^{-1}(n)
to
\bot
.
For any
\alpha\in{\mathsk{lpof}}(L)
,
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{0}
is a singleton
lpof
with
\bot
at the root.

For a set of
lpof
s
S\subseteq{\mathsk{lpof}}(L)
, we also let
\lfloor S\rfloor_{n}=\{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}\mid\alpha\in S\}
. Note that since a pomset is a set of
lpof
s, this definition applies to pomsets too.

Lemma 30
.

For any
\alpha\in{\mathsk{lpof}}(L)
and
n>0
, it holds that
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
.

Proof.

To lighten notation, let
\beta=\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}
.
First, note that
N_{\beta}
is finite, since all nodes occur at level at most
n
, and there can only be finitely many nodes up to that level.

Now, we prove that
\beta\in{\mathsk{lpof}}(L)
.
First,
\beta
is single rooted, is finitely proceeded and has finitely many nodes at every finite level, because it inherits these properties from
\alpha
. For the same reason, the conditions on
\varphi_{\beta}
are satisfied.
Finally, for any
x\in N_{\beta}
such that
\lambda_{\beta}(x)=\bot
, we consider two cases:

1.

\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)<n
: by construction,
\lambda_{\beta}(x)=\lambda_{\alpha}(x)
and so
\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)=\emptyset
. By the definition of
<_{\beta}
, we have that
\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)\subseteq\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)
and so also
\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)=\emptyset
.

2.

\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)=n
: obviously
x
has no successors, since everything above level
n
has been deleted.

Finally, we show that
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
.
First, we show that
N_{\beta}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\alpha}
. Clearly
N_{\beta}\subseteq N_{\alpha}
by construction. Now, take any
y\in N_{\beta}
and
z<_{\alpha}y
. Since
\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(y)\leq n
, then
\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(z)<n
and therefore
z\in N_{\beta}
.
By construction, we also have that
\mathord{<_{\beta}}=\mathord{<_{\alpha}}\cap(N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})
,
\lambda_{\beta}(y)\leq\lambda_{\alpha}(y)
, and
\varphi_{\beta}(y)=\varphi_{\alpha}(y)
for all
y\in N_{\beta}
. It remains only to show the condition on successors. Take any
y\in N_{\beta}
. By
Lemma

25
(3),
\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(y)\subseteq\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(y)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha}
; we now show the reverse inclusion.
Suppose that
z\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(y)
and
z\notin{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha}
. Since
y\in N_{\beta}
, then
\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(z)\leq n+1
. However, it cannot be that
\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(z)=n+1
, since that would imply that there exists
w\in\mathsf{pred}_{\alpha}(z)
such that
\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(w)=n
, but then
\lambda_{\beta}(w)=\bot
, which is a contradiction. So,
\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(z)\leq n
, which means that
z\in N_{\beta}
.
∎

An immediate consequence of
Lemma

30
is that
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}\in{\mathsk{lpof}}(L)
and
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
.

Lemma 31

(Monotonicity of truncation)
.

If
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
, then
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

26
, we have that
\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)=\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(x)
, for every
x\in N_{\alpha}\subseteq N_{\beta}
; hence,

N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\subseteq N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}
.
For downward closure, let
x\in N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}
and
y<_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}x
; thus,
y\in{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}
and again by
Lemma

26
we easily conclude.

For the order relation, we have that

\displaystyle<_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}\cap\ (N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\times N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}})

\displaystyle=(<_{\beta}\cap\ (N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}\times N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}))\cap\ (N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\times N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}})

\displaystyle=\ <_{\beta}\cap\ ((N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}\times N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}})\cap(N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\times N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}))

\displaystyle=\ <_{\beta}\cap\ (N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\times N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}})

\displaystyle=\ <_{\beta}\cap\ ((N_{\alpha}\times N_{\alpha})\cap(N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\times N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}))

\displaystyle=(<_{\beta}\cap\ (N_{\alpha}\times N_{\alpha}))\cap(N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\times N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}})

\displaystyle=\ <_{\alpha}\cap\ (N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\times N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}})=\ <_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}

where all steps follow by definition of truncation, associativity of
\cap
,
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
,
N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\subseteq N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}
and
N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\subseteq N_{\alpha}
.

Then, let
x\in N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}
. By definition and
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
, we have that
\lambda_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}(x)=\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\leq\lambda_{\beta}(x)=\lambda_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}(x)
and
\varphi_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}(x)=\varphi_{\alpha}(x)=\varphi_{\beta}(x)=\varphi_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}(x)
. By
Lemma

25
(3),
\mathsf{succ}_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}(x)\subseteq\mathsf{succ}_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}(x)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}
;
let us prove the reverse inclusion. So, let
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}(x)
; this means that
\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(x)<n
and
\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(y)\leq n
.
By assumption,
x\in N_{\alpha}
.
If also
y\in N_{\alpha}
, then
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}(x)
, because
\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(y)=\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(y)
and by
Lemma

25
(1).
If
y\notin N_{\alpha}
, then
y\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}
, because
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
; this means that there exists
z\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
such that
z<_{\beta}y
. Hence,
\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(z)=\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(z)<\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(y)=\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(y)\leq n
; thus,
z\in N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\subseteq N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}
and so
z<_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}y
. By definition of truncation,
\lambda_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}(z)=\lambda_{\alpha}(z)=\bot
; hence, we can conclude that
y\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}
.
∎

We extend the truncation to a set of lpos in the obvious way:
\lfloor D\rfloor_{n}\triangleq\{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}\mid\beta\in D\}
.

Lemma 32

(Scott continuity of truncation)
.

If
D
is a directed set of
lpof
s, then
\sup\lfloor D\rfloor_{n}
exists and it is
\lfloor\sup D\rfloor_{n}
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

6
,
\sup D=\alpha
, where:

\displaystyle N_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigcup_{\beta\in D}N_{\beta}

\displaystyle\mathord{<}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigcup_{\beta\in D}\mathord{<_{\beta}}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha}(x)

\displaystyle\triangleq\sup_{\beta\in D\,:\,x\in N_{\beta}}\lambda_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\alpha}(x)

\displaystyle\triangleq\psi_{x}

We now want to prove that
\gamma
, given by

\displaystyle N_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigcup_{\beta\in D}N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}

\displaystyle\mathord{<}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\triangleq\bigcup_{\beta\in D}\mathord{<_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\gamma}(x)

\displaystyle\triangleq\sup_{\beta\in D\,:\,x\in N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}}\lambda_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}(x)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\gamma}(x)

\displaystyle\triangleq\psi_{x}

is the sup of
\lfloor D\rfloor_{n}
and that it coincides with
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}
.

The fact that
\gamma=\sup\lfloor D\rfloor_{n}
follows by
Lemma

6
, once we prove that
\lfloor D\rfloor_{n}
is directed; so, take
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\lfloor D\rfloor_{n}
and prove that they admit an upper bound in
\lfloor D\rfloor_{n}
. By definition, there exist
\beta_{1}^{\prime},\beta_{2}^{\prime}\in D
such that
\beta_{1}=\lfloor\beta_{1}^{\prime}\rfloor_{n}
and
\beta_{2}=\lfloor\beta_{2}^{\prime}\rfloor_{n}
. Since
D
is directed, there exist a
\beta\in D
such that
\beta_{1}^{\prime},\beta_{2}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
. By
Lemma

31
,
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}
and, by definition,
\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}\in\lfloor D\rfloor_{n}
.

We are left with proving that
\gamma=\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}
.
First,
x\in N_{\gamma}
iff there exists
\beta\in D
such that
x\in N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}
, i.e.
x\in N_{\beta}
and
\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(x)=n
; this happens iff
x\in\bigcup_{\beta\in D}N_{\beta}
and
\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)=n
, being
\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)=\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(x)
(since
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
), i.e.
x\in N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}
.
Then,

\displaystyle<_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}

\displaystyle=\ <_{\alpha}\cap\ (N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\times N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}})

\displaystyle=\left(\bigcup_{\beta\in D}<_{\beta}\right)\ \cap\ (N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\times N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\beta\in D}(<_{\beta}\ \cap\ (N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\times N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}))

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\beta\in D}((<_{\alpha}\cap\ (N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta}))\ \cap\ (N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\times N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}))

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\beta\in D}(<_{\alpha}\cap\ ((N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})\ \cap\ (N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\times N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\dagger}}{{=}}\bigcup_{\beta\in D}(<_{\alpha}\cap\ (N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}\times N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ddagger}}{{=}}\bigcup_{\beta\in D}<_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}=\ <_{\gamma}

Step (
\dagger
) holds because
N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}=N_{\gamma}=\bigcup_{\beta^{\prime}\in D}N_{\lfloor\beta^{\prime}\rfloor_{n}}
and so

N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\cap N_{\beta}=(\bigcup_{\beta^{\prime}\in D}N_{\lfloor\beta^{\prime}\rfloor_{n}})\cap N_{\beta}=\bigcup_{\beta^{\prime}\in D}(N_{\lfloor\beta^{\prime}\rfloor_{n}}\cap N_{\beta})=N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}
, once we prove that
N_{\lfloor\beta^{\prime}\rfloor_{n}}\cap N_{\beta}\subseteq N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}
, for every
\beta^{\prime}\in D
(indeed, obviously
N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}\subseteq\bigcup_{\beta^{\prime}\in D}(N_{\lfloor\beta^{\prime}\rfloor_{n}}\cap N_{\beta})
). Fix
\beta^{\prime}\neq\beta
and
x\in N_{\lfloor\beta^{\prime}\rfloor_{n}}\cap N_{\beta}
;
this means that
\mathsf{lev}{\beta^{\prime}}(x)\leq n
, and so
\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(x)=\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)=\mathsf{lev}{\beta^{\prime}}(x)\leq n
, since
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
. This allows us to conclude that
x\in N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}
, as desired.
Step (
\ddagger
) is proved by double inclusion.
Trivially,
x<_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}y
means that
x<_{\beta}y
and
\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(x),\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(y)\leq n
; hence,
x,y\in N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}
and, since
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
, we also have
x<_{\alpha}y
, as desired.
Conversely, if
x<_{\alpha}y
and
x,y\in N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}
, then
x,y\in N_{\beta}
and, since
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
, also
x<_{\beta}y
; thus, by definition,
x<_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}y
.

Finally, fix
x\in N_{\gamma}=N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}
. By definition,
\varphi_{\gamma}(x)=\varphi_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}(x)
.
For the labeling, first observe that
\mathsf{lev}_{\gamma}(x)=\mathsf{lev}_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}(x)=\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)
, for every
\beta\in D
such that
x\in N_{\beta}
.
If
\mathsf{lev}_{\gamma}(x)<n
, then by construction
\lambda_{\gamma}(x)=\sup_{\beta\in D\,:\,x\in N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}}\lambda_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}(x)=\sup_{\beta\in D\,:\,x\in N_{\beta}}\lambda_{\beta}(x)=\lambda_{\alpha}(x)=\lambda_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}(x)
.
Otherwise,
x\in N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}
and
\lambda_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}(x)=\bot
, for all
\beta
such that
x\in N_{\beta}
;
hence,
\lambda_{\gamma}(x)=\sup_{\beta\in D\,:\,x\in N_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}}\lambda_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}}(x)=\bot=\lambda_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}(x)
.
∎

Appendix D
Pomsets

The following lemma provides alternative characterizations of the pomset order that will be convenient in some proofs and definitions later on.

Lemma 33
.

The following three statements are equivalent:

1.

\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}
;

2.

\exists\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha},\beta\in\boldsymbol{\beta}.\ \alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
; and

3.

\forall\beta\in\boldsymbol{\beta}.\ \exists\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}.\ \alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta

Proof.

Clearly (1) implies (2) since
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is nonempty.

We now show that (2) implies (3), assume that there exist
\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
\beta\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
such that
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
. Now, take any
\beta^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
, so
\beta\equiv\beta^{\prime}
and therefore there exists a bijection
f\colon N_{\beta}\to N_{\beta^{\prime}}
that satisfies the properties in
Definition

7
. Since
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
, then
N_{\alpha}\subseteq N_{\beta}
, and so we can construct a bijection
g
such that
g(x)=f(x)
for all
x\in N_{\alpha}
. Let
\alpha^{\prime}\equiv\alpha
be the lpo generated via the bijection
g
, so clearly
\alpha^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta^{\prime}
since both are generated by the same bijection.

Now, we show that (3) implies (1), assume that forall
\beta\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
there exists an
\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
such that
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
.
Then, take any
\alpha^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
; by definition, there exists a bijection
f:N_{\alpha}\rightarrow N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
that satisfies the properties in Definition
7
. Now, consider the LPO
\beta^{\prime}
obtained from
\beta
by replacing every
x\in N_{\alpha}
with
f(x)
and every
x\in N_{\beta}\setminus N_{\alpha}
with a node that does not occur in
