N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
.
Clearly, this induces an extension of
f
that is a bijection between
N_{\beta}
and
N_{\beta^{\prime}}
that satisfies the properties in Definition
7
; hence,
\beta^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and, trivially,
\alpha^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta^{\prime}
.
∎

D.1
Truncation

As mentioned in Appendix
C.2
, we extend the truncation operation to pomsets in the expected way:
\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rfloor_{n}\triangleq\{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}\mid\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}\}
.

Lemma 34

(Monotonicity of Truncation)
.

If
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}
, then
\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.\ \lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rfloor_{n}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\lfloor\boldsymbol{\beta}\rfloor_{n}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

33
we know that there exist
\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
\beta\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
such that
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
; by
Lemma

31
, this entails that
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\lfloor\beta\rfloor_{n}
and so, again by
Lemma

33
, that
\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rfloor_{n}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\lfloor\boldsymbol{\beta}\rfloor_{n}
.
∎

Lemma 35
.

If
\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rfloor_{n}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}
.

Proof.

(
Adapted from
[
9
, Proposition 3.1.10]
)

Fix any
\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
\beta\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
. We will show that there exists
N\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\beta}
and a bijection
f\colon N_{\alpha}\to N
such that
f(\alpha)\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
; therefore, by
Lemma

33
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}
.

We construct a tree of bijections as follows. Each node in the tree is a triple
(f,X,n)
where
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
X\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\beta}
, and
f\colon N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\to X
is a bijection such that
f(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n})\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
. We add an edge from
(f,X,n)
to
(g,Y,n+1)
if
X\subseteq Y
and
g(x)=f(x)
for all
x\in N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}
(
i.e.,

f
is
g
restricted to
N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}
or, equivalently,
g
is an extension of
f
).
Let
x_{\alpha}
and
x_{\beta}
be the roots of
\alpha
and
\beta
respectively, and
f_{0}\colon\{x_{\alpha}\}\to\{x_{\beta}\}
be the obvious bijection. We will now show that the structure we described above defines an infinite tree with
(f_{0},\{x_{\beta}\},0)
as the root.

We first show that, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a possible node with
n
as the third element; hence, there are infinitely many nodes (but we do not yet know that they can be all reached from the root). Fix any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rfloor_{n}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}=[\beta]
, then there exists an
\alpha^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
such that
\lfloor\alpha^{\prime}\rfloor_{n}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
. Since
\alpha\equiv\alpha^{\prime}
, then there must be a bijection
g\colon N_{\alpha}\to N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
. Now, let
X=\{g(x)\mid x\in N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\}
and
f\colon N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\to X
be defined as
f(x)=g(x)
, which is clearly a bijection since
g
is. Clearly

f(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n})=\lfloor g(\alpha)\rfloor_{n}=\lfloor\alpha^{\prime}\rfloor_{n}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta

and so
(f,X,n)
is a node.

We now show that any node
(f,X,n)
as described above can be reached from the root. The proof is by induction on
n
. If
n=0
, then the node must be
(f_{0},\{x_{\beta}\},0)
, hence it is the root. If
(f,X,n+1)
is a node, then
f\colon N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n+1}}\to X
is a bijection. Now, let
Y=\{f(x)\mid x\in N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\}
. So,
g\colon N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}\to Y
defined as
g(x)=f(x)
is also a bijection. We also know that
f(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n+1})\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
; since
g(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n})\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}f(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n+1})
, we have that
g(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n})\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
by transitivity. Therefore,
(g,Y,n)
is a valid node and there is an edge from
(g,Y,n)
to
(f,X,n+1)
. By the induction hypothesis,
(g,Y,n)
is reachable from the root, therefore so is
(f,X,n+1)
.

Now, let
k=|N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}|
, which must be finite since only finitely many nodes can occur at each level. Therefore, there can only be finitely many elements in each level of the tree, since there are only only finitely many downward closed subsets of
N_{\beta}
of size
k
; so, the tree must be finitely branching and, by König’s Lemma, there exists an infinite path:

(f_{n},X_{n},n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\quad\text{where}\quad X_{n}\subseteq X_{n+1}\quad\text{and}\quad\forall x\in X_{n}.\ f_{n}(x)=f_{n+1}(x)\quad\text{for all}~n\in\mathbb{N}

Now let
X=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}X_{n}
and let
f\colon N_{\alpha}\to X
be defined as
f(x)=f_{\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)}(x)
. We now show that
f(\alpha)\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
. First, clearly
N_{f(\alpha)}=X\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\beta}
since it is the union of downward closed sets. Now, for the order, we have:

\displaystyle\mathord{<_{f(\alpha)}}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathord{<_{f_{n}(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n})}}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\mathord{<_{\beta}}\cap(N_{f_{n}(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n})}\times N_{f_{n}(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n})})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathord{<_{\beta}}\cap\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(N_{f_{n}(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n})}\times N_{f_{n}(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n})}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathord{<_{\beta}}\cap(N_{f(\alpha)}\times N_{f(\alpha)})

Now, for each
x\in N_{f(\alpha)}
, let
n=\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)
. We have that:

\displaystyle\lambda_{f(\alpha)}(x)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\alpha}\left(f^{-1}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\alpha}\left(f^{-1}_{n}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\alpha}\left(f^{-1}_{n+1}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n+1}}\left(f^{-1}_{n+1}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{f_{n+1}(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n+1})}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\beta}(x)

By an analogous argument,
\varphi_{f(\alpha)}(x)=\varphi_{\beta}(x)
. Now, by
Lemma

25
, we know that
\mathsf{succ}_{f(\alpha)}(x)\subseteq\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{f(\alpha)}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}
, it just remains to show the reverse inclusion. Take any
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)
such that
y\notin{\mathsf{Bot}_{f(\alpha)}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}
. Let
m=\mathsf{lev}_{\beta}(y)
and note that
y\notin\mathsf{Bot}_{f_{m}(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{m})}
since
f(\alpha)
and
f_{m}(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{m})
are identical up to level
m
. Since
f_{m}(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{m})\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
, then
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{f_{m}(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{m})}(x)
, and therefore
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{f(\alpha)}(x)
.
∎

D.2
The Pomset Dcpo Structure

Lemma 36
.

Let
D=\{\alpha_{i}\mid i\in I\}\subseteq{\mathsk{lpof}}(L)
be a directed set and let
\boldsymbol{D}=\{[\alpha_{i}]\mid i\in I\}\subseteq{\mathsk{pom}}(L)
; then,
\sup\boldsymbol{D}
exists and
\sup\boldsymbol{D}=[\sup D]
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

6
, we know that
D
admits a sup, let us call it
\alpha
and let
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=[\alpha]
; we have to prove that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\sup\boldsymbol{D}
,
i.e.,

1.

for all
i\in I
it holds that
[\alpha_{i}]\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}[\alpha]
, and

2.

for every
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, if
[\alpha_{i}]\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}
for all
i\in I
, then
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}
.

The first property is trivial, thanks to
Lemma

33
(2).

For the second property, take some
\boldsymbol{\beta}
such that
[\alpha_{i}]\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}
for all
i\in I
and
let
A_{n}=\{\beta\in\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta\}
.

We first prove that
A_{n}\neq\emptyset
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
N=\{x\in N_{\alpha}\mid\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)\leq n\}
, which by definition is a finite set. By the definition of supremum for lpos, for each
x\in N
there exists an
i_{x}\in I
such that
x\in N_{\alpha_{i_{x}}}
and
\lambda_{\alpha_{i_{x}}}(x)=\lambda_{\alpha}(x)
. Since
\{\alpha_{i_{x}}\mid x\in N\}
is a finite subset of
D
, which is directed, there must be a
\alpha_{j}\in D
such that
\alpha_{i_{x}}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha_{j}
for all
x\in N
. By construction,
\lfloor\alpha_{j}\rfloor_{n}=\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}
. Since
\alpha_{j}\in D
, we also know that
[\alpha_{j}]\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}
, so there must be a
\beta\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
such that
\alpha_{j}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
. This gives us
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}=\lfloor\alpha_{j}\rfloor_{n}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha_{j}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
, so
\beta\in A_{n}
and therefore
A_{n}\neq\emptyset
.

Now, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, since
A_{n}\neq\emptyset
, we know that there exists a
\beta_{n}\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
such
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta_{n}
; therefore, by
Lemma

33
, we get that
\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rfloor_{n}=[\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}]\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}[\beta_{n}]=\boldsymbol{\beta}
so by
Lemma

35
, we get that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}
.
∎

Lemma 37
.

For every transfinite chain of pomsets
(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta})_{\delta<\zeta}
(such that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta^{\prime}}
if
\delta<\delta^{\prime}
), there exists a corresponding chain of
lpof
s
(\alpha_{\delta})_{\delta<\zeta}
such that
\alpha_{\delta}\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta}
, for all
\delta<\zeta
.

Proof.

We construct the chain
(\alpha_{\delta})_{\delta<\zeta}
by transfinite induction as follows.
First, fix an arbitrary
\alpha_{0}\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}
.
Second, for any ordinal
\delta
such that
\alpha_{\delta}
is fixed, we know that there exists an
\alpha_{\delta+1}\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta+1}
such that
\alpha_{\delta}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha_{\delta+1}
; so, fix this element.
Finally, for any limit ordinal
\xi
, consider
\{\alpha_{\delta}\mid\delta<\xi\}
that is a chain (thus, also a directed set); we have to choose an
\alpha_{\xi}\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\xi}
that is bigger (w.r.t.
\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}
) than any
\alpha_{\delta}
. By
Lemma

36
, we know that
\sup_{\delta<\xi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta}=[\sup_{\delta<\xi}\alpha_{\delta}]
. By definition,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\xi}
is an upper bound of
\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta}\mid\delta<\xi\}
, therefore
\sup_{\delta<\xi}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\xi}
; hence, there exists an
\alpha_{\xi}\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\xi}
such that
\sup_{\delta<\xi}\alpha_{\delta}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha_{\xi}
, which also means that
\alpha_{\delta}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha_{\xi}
for all
\delta<\xi
.
∎

Lemma 38
.

\langle{\mathsk{pom}}(L),\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\rangle
is a pointed
dcpo
.

Proof.

By
[
1
, Proposition 2.1.15]
,
\langle{\mathsk{pom}}(L),\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\rangle
is a dcpo if any transfinite chain
(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta})_{\delta<\zeta}
has a supremum. By
Lemma

37
, there is a corresponding chain of lpos
(\alpha_{\delta})_{\delta<\zeta}
such that
\alpha_{\delta}\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta}
for each
\delta<\zeta
. Therefore, by
Lemma

36
, we get that
\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta}=[\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\alpha_{\delta}]
, so the chain has a supremum.

Finally, pointedness follows from the fact that
\boldsymbol{\langle}\bot\boldsymbol{\rangle}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, for every
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, where we let

\boldsymbol{\langle}\bot\boldsymbol{\rangle}
to denote the isomorphism class of the singleton LPOs whose node is labelled with
\bot
.
∎

Appendix E
Approximation and Extension

We start by recalling the notions of approximation and compactness taken from
[
1
]
.

Definition 39

(Approximation and Compactness)
.

Given a
dcpo

\langle D,\leq\rangle
, the approximation order
\mathord{\ll}\subseteq D\times D
is defined as follows:
d_{1}\ll d_{2}
iff, for any directed set
D^{\prime}\subseteq D
such that
d_{2}\leq\sup D^{\prime}
, then
d_{1}\leq d
, for some
d\in D^{\prime}
.

An element
d\in D
is
compact
if it approximates itself (
d\ll d
).
We denote the set of compact elements of
D
as
\mathsf{K}(D)\triangleq\{d\in D\mid d\ll d\}
.

The approximation order is sometimes called the
way-below
relation or the
order of definite refinement

[
56
]
. Intuitively,
d_{1}\ll d_{2}
means that
d_{1}
is a “simpler representation” of
d_{2}
.
We now want to show that such an order coincides with the notion of finite approximations (see
Lemma

9
); this requires some preliminary results for
lpof
s.

Lemma 40
.

For any
\alpha\in{\mathsk{lpof}}(L)
, the set
{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
is directed.

Proof.

Take any
\beta,\gamma\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
, so
\beta,\gamma\in{\mathsk{lpof}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L)
and
\beta,\gamma\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
.
Let
n
be the maximum level of any node in
\beta
or
\gamma
, and
\alpha^{\prime}=\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n+1}
. Clearly,
\alpha^{\prime}\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
by
Lemma

30
.

We conclude the proof by showing that
\alpha^{\prime}
is an upper bound for
\beta
and
\gamma
; we will only show
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha^{\prime}
, as the case for
\gamma
is identical.
Clearly
N_{\beta}\subseteq N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
since
\beta
contains a subset of the nodes from
\alpha
at level at most
n
and
\alpha^{\prime}
contains all nodes from
\alpha
up to level
n+1
. We will now show that it is downward closed. Take any
x\in N_{\beta}
and
y<_{\alpha^{\prime}}x
; this means that
y<_{\alpha}x
. Being
N_{\beta}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\alpha}
, we have that
y\in N_{\beta}
.

Next, since
\alpha^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
and
N_{\beta}\subseteq N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
, we have that:

\displaystyle\mathord{<_{\alpha^{\prime}}}\cap(N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})

\displaystyle=(\mathord{<_{\alpha}}\cap(N_{\alpha^{\prime}}\times N_{\alpha^{\prime}}))\cap(N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})

\displaystyle=\mathord{<_{\alpha}}\cap((N_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cap N_{\beta})\times(N_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cap N_{\beta}))

\displaystyle=\mathord{<_{\alpha}}\cap(N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})

\displaystyle=\mathord{<_{\beta}}

Finally, let
x\in N_{\beta}
. By construction,
\varphi_{\beta}(x)=\varphi_{\alpha}(x)=\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)
and
\lambda_{\beta}(x)\leq\lambda_{\alpha}(x)=\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)
, since
\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)\leq n
.
For the last requirement, by
Lemma

25
(3) we have that
\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)\subseteq\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
; we need to prove the reverse inclusion.
Let
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)
and
y\not\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
.
We have two cases:

•

y\in N_{\beta}
: in this case,
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x)
by
Lemma

25
(1).

•

y\not\in N_{\beta}
: Because
y
is finitely proceeded in
\alpha^{\prime}
, there exist
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
such that
y_{1}=\min_{\alpha^{\prime}}
,
y_{n}=y
and
y_{i+1}\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(y_{i})
. Because of
Lemma

25
(2),
y_{i+1}\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(y_{i})
, for all
i
.
Being
y_{1}=\min_{\beta}
, there exists an
i
(
1<i<n
) such that
y_{i}\in N_{\beta}
and
y_{i+1}\not\in N_{\beta}
. Since
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
, we have that
\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(y_{i})\subseteq\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(y_{i})\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha}
; so,
y_{i+1}\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha}
and, consequently,
y\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha}
. This latter fact, together with
y\in N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
, implies that
y\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
: contradiction.
∎

Lemma 41
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in{\mathsk{pom}}(L)
, the set
{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
is directed.

Proof.

Take any
\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\in{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
, so
\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\in{\mathsk{pom}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L)
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}
. Now take any
\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, by
Lemma

33
we know that there is a
\beta\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
\beta^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}
such that
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
and
\beta^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
. Therefore,
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
, so by
Lemma

40
, there exists a
\gamma\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
such that
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
and
\beta^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\gamma
. Since
\gamma\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
, then
[\gamma]\in{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
, and clearly
\boldsymbol{\beta}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}[\gamma]
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}[\gamma]
.
∎

Lemma 42
.

For any
\alpha\in{\mathsk{lpof}}(L)
,
\alpha=\sup{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

40
,
{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
is a directed set, therefore its supremum is given by the construction in
Lemma

6
. We now show that this gives us exactly
\alpha
. First, we show this for the nodes. We know that
N_{\sup{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}}=\bigcup_{\beta\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}}N_{\beta}
.
Clearly,
\bigcup_{\beta\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}}N_{\beta}\subseteq N_{\alpha}
since each
N_{\beta}\subseteq N_{\alpha}
by definition.
For the reverse inclusion, take any
x\in N_{\alpha}
and let
n=\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(x)
. Obviously,
x\in N_{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}}
, and by
Lemma

30
, we get that
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{n}\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
. Therefore,
x\in\bigcup_{\beta\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}}N_{\beta}
, so
N_{\alpha}\subseteq\bigcup_{\beta\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}}N_{\beta}
and thus
\bigcup_{\beta\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}}N_{\beta}=N_{\alpha}

By definition,
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
, for each
\beta\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
; so clearly
\alpha
is an upper bound for
{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
and therefore
\sup{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
(since the supremum is the least upper bound). Hence,
{<_{\sup{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}}}\subseteq{<_{\alpha}}
; we show the reverse inclusion as follows. Take any
x<_{\alpha}y
and let
\gamma=\lfloor\alpha\rfloor_{\mathsf{lev}_{\alpha}(y)}
. Clearly
y\in N_{\gamma}
and by
Lemma

30
,
\gamma\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
. Since
N_{\gamma}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\alpha}
, then
x\in N_{\gamma}
too, which means that
x<_{\gamma}y
. This gives us:

\displaystyle(x,y)\quad\in\quad{<_{\gamma}}\quad\subseteq\quad\bigcup_{\beta\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}}{<_{\beta}}\quad=\quad{<_{\sup{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}}}

Finally, for any
x\in N_{\alpha}
, we have:

•

\varphi_{\sup{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}}(x)=\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
: Take any
\beta\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
, so
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\sup{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
and
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
, therefore
\varphi_{{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}}=\varphi_{\beta}=\varphi_{\alpha}
.

•

\displaystyle\lambda_{\sup{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}}(x)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\beta\in{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}:x\in N_{\beta}}\lambda_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=\sup\{\lambda_{\beta}(x)\mid\beta\in{\mathsk{lpof}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L),\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha,x\in N_{\beta}\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{=}}\sup\{\ell\mid\ell\leq\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{=}}\lambda_{\alpha}(x)

where (1) holds by the definition of
\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}
, that implies that the set above must include all the labels that are less than
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)
, and (2) holds since the set contains
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)
itself.
∎

See
9

Proof.

Fix any
\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and consider the set
{\lfloor\alpha\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
: by
Lemma

40
it is directed and by
Lemma

42
its sup is
\alpha
. Then, by
Lemma

36
,
\sup{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
exists and it is
[\alpha]
, i.e.
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
.
∎

Lemma 43
.

If
\alpha\in{\mathsk{lpof}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L)
, then there are only finitely many
\beta
such that
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
.

Proof.

Any
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
is such that
N_{\beta}\subseteq N_{\alpha}
; in other words,
N_{\beta}\in\mathcal{P}(N_{\alpha})
. If
|N_{\alpha}|=n
. then

|\mathcal{P}(N_{\alpha})|=2^{n}
(actually, there are fewer possibilities, since
N_{\beta}
is downward closed, but
2^{n}
is a sufficient upper bound).
The order and formula of
\beta
are deterministically determined by
N_{\beta}
, since
\mathord{<_{\beta}}=\mathord{<_{\alpha}}\cap(N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta})
and
\varphi_{\beta}(x)=\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
.
Let
k=\max_{x\in N_{\alpha}}|{\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{L}|
, which must be a finite integer since it is the maximum of a finite set of integers. So, there are at most
2^{n}
subsets of
N_{\alpha}
and for each of those subsets, there are at most
k
choices for the labels of each node. Hence, there are at most
2^{n}\cdot n\cdot k
possible
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
.
∎

Lemma 44
.

If
\boldsymbol{D}\subseteq{\mathsk{pom}}(L)
is a directed set, then
\lfloor\boldsymbol{D}\rfloor_{n}
is directed and finite, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

We first show that
\lfloor\boldsymbol{D}\rfloor_{n}
is directed. Take any
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\in\lfloor\boldsymbol{D}\rfloor_{n}
. This means that there is
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2}\in\boldsymbol{D}
such that
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}=\lfloor\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}\rfloor_{n}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, and since
\boldsymbol{D}
is directed, then there is a
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\boldsymbol{D}
such that each
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}
. By monotonicity of truncation, we have that
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}=\lfloor\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}\rfloor_{n}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\lfloor\boldsymbol{\gamma}\rfloor_{n}
, and clearly
\lfloor\boldsymbol{\gamma}\rfloor_{n}\in\lfloor\boldsymbol{D}\rfloor_{n}
, therefore
\lfloor\boldsymbol{D}\rfloor_{n}
is directed.

Because of
Lemma

34
, we know that
\boldsymbol{\beta}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rfloor_{n}
for all
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\lfloor\boldsymbol{D}\rfloor_{n}
.
Fix any
\alpha\in\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rfloor_{n}
, so clearly
\alpha
is finite. By
Lemma

33
, we know that, for every
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\lfloor\boldsymbol{D}\rfloor_{n}
, there is a
\beta\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
such that
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha
. By
Lemma

43
, there can only be finitely many such elements. Thus we have that
\{\beta\mid\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha\}
is finite, and also clearly
\lfloor\boldsymbol{D}\rfloor_{n}\subseteq\{[\beta]\mid\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha\}
; therefore,
\lfloor\boldsymbol{D}\rfloor_{n}
is also finite.
∎

Lemma 45
.

If
\boldsymbol{D}
is a directed set of pomsets, then
\sup\lfloor\boldsymbol{D}\rfloor_{n}=\lfloor\sup\boldsymbol{D}\rfloor_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

We show that
\lfloor-\rfloor_{n}
is chain continuous; indeed, by
[
36
, Corollary 3]
, any chain continuous function on a
dcpo
is also Scott continuous, and thus
\sup\lfloor\boldsymbol{D}\rfloor_{n}=\lfloor\sup\boldsymbol{D}\rfloor_{n}
.

Take any pomset chain
(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta})_{\delta<\zeta}
; by
Lemma

37
, there is a corresponding chain of
lpof
s
(\alpha_{\delta})_{\delta<\zeta}
such that
\alpha_{\delta}\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta}
for all
\delta<\zeta
.
Since truncation is monotonic (
Lemma

34
), then
(\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta}\rfloor_{n})_{\delta<\zeta}
is also a chain and, by
Lemma

32
, we know that
\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\lfloor\alpha_{\delta}\rfloor_{n}=\lfloor\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\alpha_{\delta}\rfloor_{n}
. Clearly
\lfloor\alpha_{\delta}\rfloor_{n}\in\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta}\rfloor_{n}
for all
\delta<\zeta
, therefore by
Lemma

36
, we get both
\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta}=[\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\alpha_{\delta}]
and
\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta}\rfloor_{n}=[\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\lfloor\alpha_{\delta}\rfloor_{n}]
. Putting all these facts together, we get:

\textstyle\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta}\rfloor_{n}=[\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\lfloor\alpha_{\delta}\rfloor_{n}]=[\lfloor\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\alpha_{\delta}\rfloor_{n}]=\lfloor[\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\alpha_{\delta}]\rfloor_{n}=\lfloor\sup_{\delta<\zeta}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\delta}\rfloor_{n}\qed

Lemma 46

(Compactness)
.

\mathsf{K}({\mathsk{pom}}(L))={\mathsk{pom}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L)
.

Proof.

We first show that any compact pomset is finite,
i.e.,

\mathsf{K}({\mathsk{pom}}(L))\subseteq{\mathsk{pom}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L)
.
Take any
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathsf{K}({\mathsk{pom}}(L))
, so for any directed set
A
, if
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\sup A
then
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}
for some
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in A
. Now, let
A={\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
, which is directed by
Lemma

41
. By
Lemma

9
, we also know that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\sup A
, so
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\sup A
. Since
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is compact, there must be some
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in A
such that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}
. Since
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in A
, then
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in{\mathsk{pom}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L)
; therefore, since
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}
, then
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in{\mathsk{pom}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L)
too.

We now show that any finite pomset is compact,
i.e.,

{\mathsk{pom}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L)\subseteq\mathsf{K}({\mathsk{pom}}(L))
. Taking any
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in{\mathsk{pom}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L)
, we must show that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}
. That is, for any directed set
A
, if
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\sup A
, then there exists
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in A
such that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}
.

To this aim, let
A
be a directed set such that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\sup A
. Since
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is finite, let
\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
be an arbitrary representative lpo, then
n=\max_{x\in N_{\alpha}}\mathsf{lev}(x)+1
must be finite. Moreover,
\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rfloor_{n}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, since
n
is one level above the maximum level of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and therefore the truncation only converts labels to
\bot
above the maximum level of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and removes nodes even above that. Therefore, by montonicity (
Lemma

34
) and Scott continuity (
Lemma

45
) of truncation, we have:

\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rfloor_{n}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\lfloor\sup A\rfloor_{n}=\sup\{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\beta}\rfloor_{n}\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in A}\}

By
Lemma

44
, we know that
\{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\beta}\rfloor_{n}\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in A}\}
is a finite directed set, and therefore it must contain its own supremum. That is, there exists
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\beta}\rfloor_{n}\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in A}\}
such that
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\sup\{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\beta}\rfloor_{n}\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in A}\}
. This means that there exists a
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\prime}\in A
such that
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\lfloor\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\prime}\rfloor_{n}
, and so clearly
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\prime}
, and therefore by transitivity,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 47

(Approximation)
.

\boldsymbol{\alpha}\ll\boldsymbol{\beta}
iff
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\beta}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\ll\boldsymbol{\beta}
. Since
{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\beta}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
is a directed set and
\sup{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\beta}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}=\boldsymbol{\beta}
(
Lemmas

41
and
9
), then there must be some
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\beta}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
such that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}
. Since
{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\beta}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
is downward closed by definition, then
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\beta}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
too.

Now suppose that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\beta}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
. By
Lemma

46
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is compact, so
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}
. By the definition of
{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\beta}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\boldsymbol{\beta}
too. Therefore, by
[
1
, Proposition 2.2.2(2)]
, we get that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\ll\boldsymbol{\beta}
.
∎

For any
dcpo
s
\langle X,\leq_{X}\rangle
and
\langle Y,\leq_{Y}\rangle
, the product poset
\langle X\times Y,\leq\rangle
where
(x,y)\leq(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
iff
x\leq_{X}x^{\prime}
and
y\leq_{Y}y^{\prime}
is also a
dcpo
[
1
, Proposition 3.2.2]
. We will use this fact in the next lemma. We write
\vec{x}\in X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}
to denote elements of Cartesian products. Let
\vec{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n})
and
\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{n})
. We write
\vec{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\ll\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
to mean that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}\ll\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.

Lemma 48
.

For any directed set
D\subseteq{\mathsk{pom}}(X)^{n}
:

\left\{\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mid\exists\vec{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in D.\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\ll\vec{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\right\}=\left\{\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\mid\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\ll\sup D\right\}

Proof.

We show the set inclusion in both directions. First, take any element
\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{n})
from the first set, so we know that
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and some
\vec{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n})\in D
. Clearly
\vec{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\sup D
, so
\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\ll\sup D

[
1
, Proposition 2.2.2(2)]
and therefore the forward inclusion holds.

Now take any element
\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{n})
from the second set and let
D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}
be the projections of
D
.
Since the supremum on the product order is coordinatewise
[
1
, Proposition 2.2.2(2)]
, then
\sup D=(\sup D_{1},\ldots,\sup D_{n})
.
So,
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}\ll\sup D_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. Therefore, by the definition of
\ll
, there must be some
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}\in D_{i}
such that
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}
. Now, for each
i
, let
\vec{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}\in D
be the tuple whose
i-
th projection is
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}
.
Since
D
is directed, then there is some
\vec{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in D
, which is an upper bound of all the
\vec{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}
s.
From
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}\ll\sup D_{i}
and
Corollary

47
, we know that
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}
is finite, therefore
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}\in{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
. By
Corollary

47
again,
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}
, and so
\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and the reverse inclusion holds too.
∎

See
10

Proof.

By
Corollary

47
,
\{(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}^{\prime})\mid\forall i.\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}^{\prime}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}\}={\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}\times\cdots\times{\lfloor\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}\rfloor}_{\mathsf{fin}}
, that is a directed set by
Lemma

41
. Since
f
is monotone, then
\{f(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}^{\prime})\mid\forall i.\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}^{\prime}\ll\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}\}
is also a directed set, and so clearly the supremum exists.

We now establish that
f^{\ast}
is Scott continuous. Let
D\subseteq{\mathsk{pom}}(L)^{n}
be a directed set and:

\displaystyle\sup_{\vec{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in D}f^{\ast}(\vec{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\vec{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in D}\sup_{\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\ll\vec{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}f(\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}})

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{f(\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\mid\exists\vec{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in D.\,\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\ll\vec{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\right\}

By
[
1
, Proposition 2.1.4(3)]
.

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{f(\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\mid\vec{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\ll\sup D\right\}

By
Lemma

48
.

\displaystyle=f^{\ast}(\sup D)

∎

Appendix F
Operations

F.1
Sequential Composition

Lemma 49

(Antitonicity of
\mathsf{stuck}
)
.

If
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
, then
\mathsf{stuck}_{\beta}\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

For any
z\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}
, either
z\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
or
z\notin N_{\alpha}
. In the first case,
\varphi_{\beta}(z)=\varphi_{\alpha}(z)
, being
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
; in the second case, by
Lemma

23
, there is some
w\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
such that
w<_{\beta}z
, which implies that
\varphi_{\beta}(z)\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(w)
. This means that every term in
\bigvee_{z\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}}\varphi_{\beta}(z)
implies some term in
\bigvee_{y\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}}\varphi_{\alpha}(y)
, therefore
\bigvee_{z\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}}\varphi_{\beta}(z)\Rightarrow\bigvee_{y\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}}\varphi_{\alpha}(y)
.
∎

Lemma 50

(Monotonicity of
\mathsf{ext}
)
.

If
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
, then
\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha}=\{x\in\mathsf{ext}_{\beta}\mid\varphi_{\beta}(x)\not\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}\}
.

Proof.

We show the inclusion in both directions.

Take any
x\in\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha}
, this means that
x\in N_{\alpha}
and
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\not\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
; so, there is a valuation
v
such that
v\vDash\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
and
v\not\vDash\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
. By
Lemma

49
,
\mathsf{stuck}_{\beta}\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
, therefore
v
cannot satisfy
\mathsf{stuck}_{\beta}
either. Since
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)=\varphi_{\beta}(x)
(because
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
), we have that

\varphi_{\beta}(x)\not\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
and that

v\vDash\varphi_{\beta}(x)
and
v\not\vDash\mathsf{stuck}_{\beta}
; therefore,
\varphi_{\beta}(x)\not\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\beta}
and so
x\in\mathsf{ext}_{\beta}
.

For the reverse inclusion, take any
x\in\mathsf{ext}_{\beta}
such that
\varphi_{\beta}(x)\not\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
. By
Lemma

23
, we know that either
x\in N_{\alpha}
or
x\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}
. In the former case, we know that
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)=\varphi_{\beta}(x)\not\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
, therefore
x\in\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha}
. The latter case is a contradiction, since
x\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}
means that there is some
y\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
such that
y<_{\beta}x
, which entails that
\varphi_{\beta}(x)\Rightarrow\varphi_{\beta}(y)
; however,
\varphi_{\beta}(y)=\varphi_{\alpha}(y)\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
and, by combining these facts, we get that
\varphi_{\beta}(x)\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
.∎

Lemma 51

(Monotonicity of
\mathsf{br}
)
.

If
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
, then
\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}=\{\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\beta}\mid\psi\Rightarrow\lnot\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}\}
.

Proof.

We show the inclusion in both directions.

For the forward inclusion, take
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
, i.e.
\psi=\varphi_{\alpha}(S)
, for some
S\subseteq_{\mathsf{fin}}\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha}
maximally satisfiable and that does not imply
\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
; we want to show that
\psi=\varphi_{\beta}(S)\in\mathsf{br}_{\beta}
.
By
Lemma

50
, we know that
\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathsf{ext}_{\beta}
, therefore
S\subseteq_{\mathsf{fin}}\mathsf{ext}_{\beta}
. Further, since
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)=\varphi_{\beta}(x)
for all
x\in N_{\alpha}
, then
\varphi_{\beta}(S)=\varphi_{\alpha}(S)\Rightarrow\lnot\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
; by using the contrapositive of
Lemma

49
, we get that
\varphi_{\beta}(S)\Rightarrow\lnot\mathsf{stuck}_{\beta}
.
Last, take any
T
such that
S\subset T\subseteq\mathsf{ext}_{\beta}
. If
T\subseteq\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha}
, we already know that
\varphi_{\alpha}(T)
cannot be satisfiable; so suppose that there is some node
x\in T
such that
x\notin\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha}
, meaning that either
x\notin N_{\alpha}
or
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
.
In the first case, by
Lemma

23

x\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}
, so there is a
z\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
such that
z<_{\beta}x
, which also means that
\varphi_{\beta}(x)\Rightarrow\varphi_{\beta}(z)\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
. So, in either case, we have
\varphi_{\beta}(x)\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
, while, since
\varphi_{\alpha}(S)\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
, we know that
\varphi_{\alpha}(S)\Rightarrow\lnot\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
. These give us:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\beta}(T)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\varphi_{\beta}(S)\land\varphi_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\alpha}(S)\land\varphi_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\lnot\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}\land\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathsf{false}

So,
\varphi_{\beta}(T)
is not satisfiable, and therefore we have shown that
\varphi_{\beta}(S)\in\mathsf{br}_{\beta}
(and, clearly,
\varphi_{\beta}(S)\Rightarrow\lnot\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
).

For the reverse inclusion, take any
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\beta}
such that
\psi\Rightarrow\lnot\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
. We know that
\psi=\varphi_{\beta}(S)
where
S\subseteq\mathsf{ext}_{\beta}
. By
Lemma

50
,
for each
x\in S
, either
x\in\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha}
or
\varphi_{\beta}(x)\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
. But the latter case is not possible, otherwise we would be able to contradict
\varphi_{\beta}(S)=\psi\Rightarrow\lnot\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
. Therefore,
S\subseteq\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha}
. Now take any
T
such that
S\subset T\subseteq_{\mathsf{fin}}\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha}
. Since
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\beta}
, we know that
\varphi_{\beta}(T)
is not satisfiable and, since
\varphi_{\alpha}(T)=\varphi_{\beta}(T)
, then
\varphi_{\alpha}(T)
is not satisfiable either. Therefore,
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
.
∎

Lemma 52

(Monotonicity of
\fatsemi
for
lpof
s)
.

For all
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathsk{lpof}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L)
and
f\in\mathsf{copy}_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
such that
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta^{\prime}
, there exists
g\in\mathsf{copy}_{\alpha,\beta}
such that
\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}=f(\psi)
for each
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
. Since
\beta^{\prime}\equiv f(\psi)
for all
\psi
, then for each
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
there exists a bijection
f_{\psi}\colon N_{\beta^{\prime}}\to N_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}
. Since
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta^{\prime}
, then
N_{\beta}\subseteq N_{\beta^{\prime}}
; so, we can restrict
f_{\psi}
to
N_{\beta}
and obtain an lpo-isomorphism
g_{\psi}
(that clearly satisfies
g_{\psi}(x)=f_{\psi}(x)
for all
x\in N_{\beta}
). Now, let
\beta_{\psi}
be an isomorphic copy of
\beta
generated by the bijection
g_{\psi}
. Clearly,
N_{\beta_{\psi}}\cap N_{\alpha}=\emptyset
, since
N_{\alpha}\subseteq N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
and
N_{\beta_{\psi}}\subseteq N_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}
; similarly, all the
\beta_{\psi}
s have pairwise disjoint nodes. By
Lemma

51
,
\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathsf{br}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
; so, we have such a
\beta_{\psi}
for all
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
, and we can define
g\in\mathsf{copy}_{\alpha,\beta}
as
g(\psi)=\beta_{\psi}
for all
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
.
Also,
\beta_{\psi}=g_{\psi}(\beta)\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}f_{\psi}(\beta^{\prime})=\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}
for all
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
, since
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta^{\prime}
and
g_{\psi}\subseteq f_{\psi}
.

We start with proving that
N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}
. Take any
x\in N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}
; so, either
x\in N_{\alpha}(\subseteq N_{\alpha^{\prime}})
or
x\in N_{\beta_{\psi}}(\subseteq N_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}})
, for some
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
. Clearly
x\in N_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}
, so the subset inclusion holds; we now show that it is downward closed. Take any
y<_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}x
.
If
x\in N_{\alpha}
, then clearly
y<_{\alpha^{\prime}}x
, since
\fatsemi
does not add any causality into elements of
N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
, and we therefore conclude that
y\in N_{\alpha}\subseteq N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}
, since
N_{\alpha}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
.
If instead
x\in N_{\beta_{\psi}}
for some
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
, then we know by
Lemma

51
that
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
and therefore
x\in N_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}\subseteq N_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}
. Since
y<_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}x
, then either
y<_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}x
, or
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(y)
. In the former case, then clearly
y\in N_{\beta_{\psi}}\subseteq N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}
since
N_{\beta_{\psi}}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}
. In the latter case, suppose for the sake of contradiction that
y\notin N_{\alpha}
; then, by
Lemma

23
there is some
z\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
such that
z<_{\alpha^{\prime}}y
, which also means that
\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(y)\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(z)
. Since
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha^{\prime}
and
z\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
, we have that
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(y)\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(z)\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(z)\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
; this contradicts
\psi\Rightarrow\lnot\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
, that holds since
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
.

Next, we show the condition on the order:

\displaystyle\mathord{<_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}}\cap(N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}\times N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta})

\displaystyle\qquad=\left(\mathord{<_{\alpha^{\prime}}}\cup\bigcup_{\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha^{\prime}}}\left(\mathord{<_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}}\cup(\{x\mid\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)\}\times N_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}})\right)\right)\cap(N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}\times N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta})

\displaystyle\qquad=\left(\mathord{<_{\alpha^{\prime}}}\cap(N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}\times N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\cup\bigcup_{\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha^{\prime}}}\left(\mathord{<_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}}\cap(N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}\times N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta})\right)\cup\left((\{x\mid\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)\}\times N_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}})\cap(N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}\times N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta})\right)\qquad(\dagger)

Now,
N_{\alpha}
is a subset of
N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
and it is disjoint from all the
N_{\beta_{\psi}}
; moreover,
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha^{\prime}
. So,

\mathord{<_{\alpha^{\prime}}}\cap(N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}\times N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta})=\mathord{<_{\alpha^{\prime}}}\cap(N_{\alpha}\times N_{\alpha})=\mathord{<_{\alpha}}

Then, by
Lemma

51

\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathsf{br}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
, and, for all
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\setminus\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
, the nodes of
\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}
do not appear in
N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}
; hence, we can reduce the bounds of the union in (
\dagger
) and obtain:

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha^{\prime}}}\left(\mathord{<_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}}\cap(N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}\times N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta})\right)\cup\left((\{x\mid\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)\}\times N_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}})\cap(N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}\times N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\bigcup_{\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}}\left(\mathord{<_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}}\cap(N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}\times N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta})\right)\cup\left((\{x\mid\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)\}\times N_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}})\cap(N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}\times N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\bigcup_{\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}}\left(\mathord{<_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}}\cap(N_{\beta_{\psi}}\times N_{\beta_{\psi}})\right)\cup(\{x\mid\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)\}\times N_{\beta_{\psi}})

\displaystyle\qquad=\bigcup_{\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}}\left(\mathord{<_{\beta_{\psi}}}\cup(\{x\mid\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\}\times N_{\beta_{\psi}})\right)

where the last two equalities hold because
\beta_{\psi}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}
and because the nodes of
\beta_{\psi}^{\prime}
are disjoint from those of
\alpha
and of any other isomorphic copy of
\beta
.
Thus, (
\dagger
) is equal to
\mathord{<_{\alpha}}\cup\bigcup_{\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}}\left(\mathord{<_{\beta_{\psi}}}\cup(\{x\mid\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\}\times N_{\beta_{\psi}})\right)
that, by definition, is
\mathord{<_{\alpha\fatsemi\beta}}
.

Now, take any
x\in N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}
.
Since
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta_{\psi}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta_{\psi}^{\prime}
, we have that
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\leq\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)
, if
x\in N_{\alpha}
, and
\lambda_{\beta_{\psi}}(x)\leq\lambda_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}(x)
, if
x\in N_{\beta_{\psi}}
. This gives us:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\lambda_{\alpha}(x)&\text{if}~x\in N_{\alpha}\\
\lambda_{\beta_{\psi}}(x)&\text{if}~x\in N_{\beta_{\psi}}\end{array}\right\}\ \leq\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)&\text{if}~x\in N_{\alpha}\\
\lambda_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}(x)&\text{if}~x\in N_{\beta_{\psi}}\end{array}\right\}=\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}(x)

Similarly, we have that
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)=\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)
, if
x\in N_{\alpha}
, and
\varphi_{\beta_{\psi}}(x)=\varphi_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}(x)
, if
x\in N_{\beta_{p}si}
; so

\displaystyle\varphi_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\varphi_{\alpha}(x)&\text{if}~x\in N_{\alpha}\\
\varphi_{\beta_{\psi}}(x)\land\psi&\text{if}~x\in N_{\beta_{\psi}}\end{array}\right\}\ =\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)&\text{if}~x\in N_{\alpha}\\
\varphi_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}(x)\land\psi&\text{if}~x\in N_{\beta_{\psi}}\end{array}\right\}=\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}(x)

For successors, we know by
Lemma

25
(3) that
\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}(x)\subseteq\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}(x)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}
, we now must show the reverse inclusion.
Take any
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}(x)
such that
y\notin{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}
. Given the way that
<_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}
is constructed, there are three cases to consider:

1.

y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)
. Since
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha^{\prime}
, we know that
\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)=\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
. However,
y\notin{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
(since
{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}
); therefore, it must be that
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(x)
, and so
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}(x)
.

2.

y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}(x)
for some
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
. This means that
x,y\in N_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}
. Let
x^{\prime}=f_{\psi}^{-1}(x)
and
y^{\prime}=f_{\psi}^{-1}(y)
, so
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in N_{\beta^{\prime}}
.
Since
\beta^{\prime}\equiv\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}
, we know that
y^{\prime}\in\mathsf{succ}_{\beta^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})
. Since
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta^{\prime}
, then
\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x^{\prime})=\mathsf{succ}_{\beta^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta^{\prime}}
. However, since
y\notin{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha\fatsemi\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi\beta^{\prime}}
, then it must be that
y^{\prime}\notin{\mathsf{Bot}_{\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta^{\prime}}
; so we must have that
y^{\prime}\in\mathsf{succ}_{\beta}(x^{\prime})
. Since
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in N_{\beta}
, then
g_{\psi}(x^{\prime})=f_{\psi}(x^{\prime})=x
and
g_{\psi}(y^{\prime})=f_{\psi}(y^{\prime})=y
; so,
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\beta_{\psi}}(x)
and therefore
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}(x)
.

3.

x\in N_{\alpha}
and
y\in N_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}
for some
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
.
By
Lemma

51
, either
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
or
\psi\not\Rightarrow\lnot\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
.

For the first case,
x<_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}y
,
x\in N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
and
y\in N_{\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}}
imply that
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)=\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
. Furthermore,
y
must be the root of
\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}
, otherwise it could not be a successor of
x\in N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
. Since
\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}
is single-rooted and
\beta_{\psi}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta^{\prime}_{\psi}
, then
y
must also be the root of
\beta_{\psi}
; so,
x<_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}y
and, by
Lemma

25
(1),
y\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}(x)
.

To conclude, we show that the second case is impossible. Suppose that
\psi\not\Rightarrow\lnot\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
.
From
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
we also know that
\psi\Rightarrow\lnot\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
. Therefore, there is some
v
such that
v\vDash\psi
,
v\vDash\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
and
v\not\vDash\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
. From
v\vDash\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
, we get that
v\vDash\varphi_{\alpha}(z)
, for some
z\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
; so
v\vDash\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(z)
too (since
\varphi_{\alpha}(z)=\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(z)
).
Since
v\vDash\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(z)
and
v\not\vDash\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
, then
\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(z)\not\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
; therefore
z\in\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
.
Moreover, since
v\vDash\psi
and
v\vDash\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(z)
, we know that
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(z)
is satisfiable; since
S
is maximal, then
z\in S
. Therefore,
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(z)
, and so
z<_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}y
(by the definition of
<_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}
), in contradiction with
y\notin{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}}
.
∎

Lemma 53

(Monotonicity of
\fatsemi
)
.

If
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}
, then
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\beta}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}

Proof.

Take any
\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}
; by construction,
\gamma=\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}
, for some
\alpha^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}
,
\beta^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}
, and
f\in\mathsf{copy}_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
. By
Lemma

33
(3), there exist
\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
\beta\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
such that
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta^{\prime}
. So, by
Lemma

52
there exists a
g\in\mathsf{copy}_{\alpha,\beta}
such that
\alpha\fatsemi_{g}\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha^{\prime}\fatsemi_{f}\beta^{\prime}
. So, by
Lemma

33
(3)
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\beta}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\fatsemi\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 54
.

\fatsemi
is Scott continuous.

Proof.

Immediate from
Lemmas

10
and
53
.
∎

F.2
Guarded Branching

Lemma 55

(Monotonicity of
\mathsf{guard}
for
lpof
s)
.

For every
\ell\in L
,
x\in\mathsk{nodes}
and
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathsk{lpof}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L)
such that
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha^{\prime}
,
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta^{\prime}
,
N_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cap N_{\beta^{\prime}}=\emptyset
and
x\not\in N_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cup N_{\beta^{\prime}}
, it holds that
\mathsf{guard}(x,\ell,\alpha,\beta)\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\mathsf{guard}(x,\ell,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
.

Proof.

To lighten notations, let us fix
\ell
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\beta,\beta^{\prime}
and
x
,
and denote
\gamma\triangleq\mathsf{guard}(x,\ell,\alpha,\beta)
and
\gamma^{\prime}\triangleq\mathsf{guard}(x,\ell,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
.

First, being
N_{\alpha}\subseteq N_{\beta}
and
N_{\alpha^{\prime}}\subseteq N_{\beta^{\prime}}
, we also have that

N_{\gamma}=\{x\}\cup N_{\alpha}\cup N_{\beta}\subseteq\{x\}\cup N_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cup N_{\beta^{\prime}}=N_{\gamma^{\prime}}
;
we have to prove that
N_{\gamma}
is downward closed.
Let
y\in N_{\gamma}
and
z<_{\gamma^{\prime}}y
; clearly,
y\neq x
, because
\mathsf{pred}_{\gamma^{\prime}}(x)=\emptyset
.
Assume that
y\in N_{\alpha}
(
\subseteq N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
); the case for
y\in N_{\beta}
is identical.
If
z=x
, we trivially conclude. If
z\in N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
, we have
z<_{\alpha^{\prime}}y
, which leads to
z\in N_{\alpha}
(because
N_{\alpha}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
); hence,
z\in N_{\gamma}
.

Second,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lx@intercol<_{\gamma^{\prime}}\cap\ (N_{\gamma}\times N_{\gamma})\hfil\lx@intercol\vskip 4.26773pt\\
&=&(<_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cup<_{\beta^{\prime}}\cup\ (\{x\}\times(N_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cup N_{\beta^{\prime}})))\ \cap\ (N_{\gamma}\times N_{\gamma})\\
&=&(<_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cap\ ((\{x\}\cup N_{\alpha}\cup N_{\beta})\times(\{x\}\cup N_{\alpha}\cup N_{\beta})))\\
&&\cup\ (<_{\beta^{\prime}}\cap\ ((\{x\}\cup N_{\alpha}\cup N_{\beta})\times(\{x\}\cup N_{\alpha}\cup N_{\beta})))\\
&&\cup\ ((\{x\}\times(N_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cup N_{\beta^{\prime}}))\cap((\{x\}\cup N_{\alpha}\cup N_{\beta})\times(\{x\}\cup N_{\alpha}\cup N_{\beta})))\\
&=&(<_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cap\ (N_{\alpha}\times N_{\alpha}))\ \cup\ (<_{\beta^{\prime}}\cap\ (N_{\beta}\times N_{\beta}))\ \cup\ (\{x\}\times(N_{\alpha}\cup N_{\beta}))\\
&=&<_{\alpha}\cup<_{\beta}\cup\ (\{x\}\times(N_{\alpha}\cup N_{\beta}))\ \ =\ \ <_{\gamma}\end{array}

Fix
y\in N_{\gamma}
; we have three subcases to consider:

1.

y=x
: by construction,
\lambda_{\gamma}(y)=\lambda_{\gamma^{\prime}}(y)=\ell
and
\varphi_{\gamma}(y)=\varphi_{\gamma^{\prime}}=\mathsf{true}
.

2.

y\in N_{\alpha}
: then,
\lambda_{\gamma}(y)=\lambda_{\alpha}(y)\leq\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime}}(y)=\lambda_{\gamma^{\prime}}(y)
and

\varphi_{\gamma}(y)=\varphi_{\alpha}(y)\wedge x=\varphi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(y)\wedge x=\lambda_{\gamma^{\prime}}(y)
.

3.

y\in N_{\beta}
: this case is similar to previous one, with
\neg x
in place of
x
for the
\varphi
case.

To conclude, we need to prove that
\mathsf{succ}_{\gamma}(y)\supseteq\mathsf{succ}_{\gamma^{\prime}}(y)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\gamma}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
(since the other inclusion holds for
Lemma

25
). Thus, take
z\in\mathsf{succ}_{\gamma^{\prime}}(y)
; we have three cases to consider:

1.

y=x
: by construction,
z\in\{\min_{\alpha^{\prime}},\min_{\beta^{\prime}}\}
and so we immediately conclude
z\in\mathsf{succ}_{\gamma}(y)
, being
\min_{\alpha}=\min_{\alpha^{\prime}}
and
\min_{\beta}=\min_{\beta^{\prime}}
.

2.

y\in N_{\alpha}
: by construction,
z\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(y)
.
Since
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha^{\prime}
, we know that
\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(y)=\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(y)\setminus{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
.
So, either
z\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(y)=\mathsf{succ}_{\gamma}(y)
or
z\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Bot}_{\gamma}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
.

3.

y\in N_{\beta}
: this case is similar to previous one.
∎

Lemma 56

(Monotonicity of
\mathsf{guard}
)
.

If
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}
, then

\mathsf{guard}(\ell,\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\mathsf{guard}(\ell,\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Take any
\gamma\in\mathsf{guard}(\ell,\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime})
; by construction,
\gamma=\mathsf{guard}(x,\ell,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
, for some
\alpha^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}
such that
N_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cap N_{\beta^{\prime}}=\emptyset
and
x\notin N_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cup N_{\beta^{\prime}}
. By
Lemma

33
(3), there exist
\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
\beta\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
such that
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta^{\prime}
. So, by
Lemma

55

\mathsf{guard}(x,\ell,\alpha,\beta)\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\mathsf{guard}(x,\ell,\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
. So, by
Lemma

33
(3)
\mathsf{guard}(\ell,\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})\sqsubseteq_{{\mathsk{pom}}}\mathsf{guard}(\ell,\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime})
.
∎

Corollary 57
.

\mathsf{guard}
is Scott continuous.

Proof.

Immediate from
Lemmas

56
and
10
.
∎

Appendix G
Denotational Semantics

G.1
Linearization

Lemma 58
.

If
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
and
S\cap\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}=\emptyset
, then
\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi,S)=\mathsf{next}(\beta,\psi,S)
.

Proof.

We show the set inclusion in both directions. Take any
y\in\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi,S)
, so
y\in N_{\alpha}
,
y\notin S
,
{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha}\subseteq S
, and
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(y)
. Since
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
, we get that
y\in N_{\beta}
,
{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha}={y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta}
, and
\varphi_{\alpha}(y)=\varphi_{\beta}(y)
, so
y\in\mathsf{next}(\beta,\psi,S)
.

For the reverse inclusion, take any
y\in\mathsf{next}(\beta,\psi,S)
, so
y\in N_{\beta}
,
y\notin S
,
{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta}\subseteq S
, and
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\beta}(y)
. If
y\in N_{\alpha}
, then the proof follows from a similar argument as above. If not, we will show that there is a contradiction. Suppose that
y\notin N_{\alpha}
, so by
Lemma

23
, we get that
y\in{\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}\mathclose{\uparrow}}_{\beta}
. So there is a
z\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
such that
z<_{\beta}y
. Since
{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\beta}\subseteq S
, then
z\in S
. However, this is a contradiction since we assumed that
S\cap\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}=\emptyset
.
∎

For any set
X
, the pointwise extension
\langle X\to D(\mathcal{S}),\sqsubseteq^{\bullet}\rangle
is also a pointed
dcpo
, where
f\sqsubseteq^{\bullet}g
iff
f(x)\sqsubseteq g(x)
for all
x\in X
. We use this fact below.

Lemma 59

(Monotonicity of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}
)
.

For any
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathsk{lpof}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L)
,
\psi\in\mathsk{form}
,
S\subseteq N_{\alpha}
, and
s\in\mathcal{S}
such that
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
and
S\cap\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}=\emptyset
:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\psi,S)\sqsubseteq^{\bullet}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\beta,\psi,S)

Proof.

Let
T_{S,\psi}=\{x\in N_{\alpha}\mid x\notin S,\mathsf{sat}(\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(x))\}
be the set of nodes still to schedule. The proof is by induction on the size of the set
T_{S,\psi}
.

If
T_{S,\psi}
is empty, then
x\in S
or
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
is unsatisfiable for all
x\in N_{\alpha}
. In either case, clearly
x\notin\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi,S)
, therefore
\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi,S)=\emptyset
. Moreover, by
Lemma

58
,
\mathsf{next}(\beta,\psi,S)=\emptyset
too and so
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\psi,S)(s)=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\beta,\psi,S)(s)=\eta(s)
.

Now suppose the claim holds for all sets smaller than
T_{S,\psi}
, and let
f
be defined as follows:

f(\gamma,\psi,S,x)(s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\gamma,\psi,S\cup\{x\})^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket\lambda_{\gamma}(x)\right\rrbracket{act}(s))&\text{if}~\lambda_{\gamma}(x)\in\mathsk{act}\\
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\gamma,\psi\land\llparenthesis x=\left\llbracket\lambda_{\gamma}(x)\right\rrbracket(s)\rrparenthesis,S\cup\{x\})(s)&\text{if}~\lambda_{\gamma}(x)\in\mathsk{test}\\
\bot&\text{if}~\lambda_{\gamma}(x)=\bot\\
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\gamma,\psi,S\cup\{x\})(s)&\text{if}~\lambda_{\gamma}(x)=\mathsf{fork}\end{array}\right.

So,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\gamma,\psi,S)(s)=\bignd_{x\in\mathsf{next}(\gamma,\psi,S)}f(\gamma,\psi,S,x)(s)
. We start by showing that
f(\alpha,\psi,S,x)(s)\sqsubseteq f(\beta,\psi,S,x)(s)
. First, we remark that
S\cap\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}=\emptyset
and so, if
x\notin\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
, then
(S\cup\{x\})\cap\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}=\emptyset
, which will allow us to use the induction hypothesis in cases (1), (2), and (4) below. We proceed by case analysis on
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)
.

1.

If
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\in\mathsk{act}
, then
\lambda_{\beta}(x)\in\mathsk{act}
too, and
\left\llbracket\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\right\rrbracket{act}(s)\sqsubseteq\left\llbracket\lambda_{\beta}(x)\right\rrbracket{act}(s)
. Note that
T_{S\cup\{x\},\psi}\subset T_{S,\psi}
, so by the induction hypothesis, we can conclude that:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\psi,S\cup\{x\})\sqsubseteq^{\bullet}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\beta,\psi,S\cup\{x\})

Therefore, by monotonicity of Kleisli extension, we get that:

\displaystyle f(\alpha,\psi,S,x)(s)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\psi,S\cup\{x\})^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\right\rrbracket{act}(s))

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\beta,\psi,S\cup\{x\})^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket\lambda_{\beta}(x)\right\rrbracket{act}(s))

\displaystyle=f(\beta,\psi,S,x)(s)

2.

If
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\in\mathsk{test}
, then again
\lambda_{\beta}(x)\in\mathsk{test}
as well and
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)=\lambda_{\beta}(x)
since the order over tests is flat. Since
T_{S\cup\{x\},\psi\land\llparenthesis x=\left\llbracket b\right\rrbracket{test}(s)\rrparenthesis}\subset T_{S,\psi}
, then we can use the induction hypothesis to conclude that:

\displaystyle f(\alpha,\psi,S,x)(s)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\psi\land\llparenthesis x=\left\llbracket\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\right\rrbracket{test}(s)\rrparenthesis,S\cup\{x\})(s)

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\beta,\psi\land\llparenthesis x=\left\llbracket\lambda_{\beta}(x)\right\rrbracket{test}(s)\rrparenthesis,S\cup\{x\})(s)

\displaystyle=f(\beta,\psi,S,x)(s)

3.

If
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)=\bot
, then we have:

f(\alpha,\psi,S,x)(s)\quad=\quad\bot\quad\sqsubseteq\quad f(\beta,\psi,S,x)(s)

4.

If
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)=\mathsf{fork}
, then
\lambda_{\beta}(x)=\mathsf{fork}
, and so since
T_{S\cup\{x\},\psi}\subset T_{S,\psi}
, the induction hypothesis gives us the following:

f(\alpha,\psi,S,x)(x)\;\;=\;\;\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\psi,S\cup\{x\})(x)\;\;\sqsubseteq\;\;\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\beta,\psi,S\cup\{x\})(x)\;\;=\;\;f(\beta,\psi,S,x)(x)

We now complete the proof of the main claim as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\psi,S)(s)

\displaystyle=\bignd_{x\in\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi,S)}f(\alpha,\psi,S,x)(s)

Since
S\cap\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}=\emptyset
, by
Lemma

58
we know that
\mathsf{next}(\alpha,\psi,S)=\mathsf{next}(\beta,\psi,S)
.

\displaystyle=\bignd_{x\in\mathsf{next}(\beta,\psi,S)}f(\alpha,\psi,S,x)(s)

Since
\mathbin{\&}
is monotone, and given the claim we just proved about
f
:

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\bignd_{x\in\mathsf{next}(\beta,\psi,S)}f(\beta,\psi,S,x)(s)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\beta,\psi,S)(s)\qed

Lemma 60

(Monotonicity of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}
)
.

If
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\boldsymbol{\beta}
, then
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\sqsubseteq^{\bullet}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}(\boldsymbol{\beta})
.

Proof.

Fix any
\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, then we know there exists a
\beta\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
such that
\alpha\sqsubseteq_{\mathsk{lpof}}\beta
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}([\alpha])

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\mathsf{true},\emptyset)

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq^{\bullet}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\beta,\mathsf{true},\emptyset)

By
Lemma

59

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}([\beta])

Corollary 61
.

\mathcal{L}\colon{\mathsk{pom}}\to(\mathcal{S}\to D(\mathcal{S}))
is Scott continuous.

Proof.

Immediate from
Lemmas

10
and
60
.
∎

G.2
Properties of Linearization: Skip and Actions

Lemma 62
.

\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket\textsf{skip}\right\rrbracket)=\eta
and
\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket)=\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket{act}
.

Proof.

We show only the case for
skip
, as the case for actions is nearly identical.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\left\llbracket\textsf{skip}\right\rrbracket)(s)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathsf{fork}\boldsymbol{\rangle})(s)

Since a singleton pomset is already finite,
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}
. Fixing an arbitrary
x\in\mathsk{nodes}
, we have:

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}([\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathsf{fork}\boldsymbol{\rangle}_{x}])(s)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathsf{fork}\boldsymbol{\rangle}_{x},\mathsf{true},\emptyset)(s)

Obviously,
(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathsf{fork}\boldsymbol{\rangle}_{x},\mathsf{true},\emptyset)=\{x\}
, so we get:

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\mathsf{fork}\boldsymbol{\rangle}_{x},\mathsf{true},\{x\})(s)

\displaystyle=\eta(s)\qed

G.3
Properties of Linearization: Sequential Composition

We first introduce a few definitions:

{}^{\ast}(\alpha,\psi,S)

\displaystyle=\{x\in N_{\alpha}\setminus S\mid\mathsf{sat}(\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(x))\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{tests}_{\alpha}(S)

\displaystyle=\{x\in S\mid\lambda_{\alpha}(x)\in\mathsk{test}\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{forms}(X)

\displaystyle=\left\{\bigwedge_{x\in X}\llparenthesis x=b_{x}\rrparenthesis~\middle|~\forall x\in X.\ b_{x}\in\mathbb{B}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{valid}_{\alpha}(S)

\displaystyle=\{\psi\mid\psi\in\mathsf{forms}(\mathsf{tests}_{\alpha}(S)),\forall x\in S.\ \psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\}

The set
{}^{*}(\alpha,\psi,S)
contains all of the nodes in
\alpha
that are still able to be scheduled, given that
S
has already been processed and
\psi
is the path condition. The set
\mathsf{tests}_{\alpha}(S)
contains all the test nodes from
S
. The set
\mathsf{forms}(X)
contains all the formulae over the variables
X
, which are conjunctions of literals, where a literal is either a variable
x
or its negation
\lnot x
. Finally,
\mathsf{valid}_{\alpha}(S)
contains all the formulae over
\mathsf{tests}_{\alpha}(S)
that imply the formulae of all
x\in S
.
As an abuse of notation, we will write
\psi\in S
to mean that there exists a
\psi^{\prime}\in S
such that
\psi\Leftrightarrow\psi^{\prime}
.

Lemma 63
.

If
\alpha
is binary branching,
S\subseteq\mathsf{nBot}_{\alpha}
,
{}^{*}(\alpha,\psi,S)=\emptyset
and
\psi\in\mathsf{valid}_{\alpha}(S)
, then
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
and
(\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta)=\min_{f(\psi)}
.

Proof.

Since
{}^{*}(\alpha,\psi,S)=\emptyset
, we know that for all
x\in N_{\alpha}
, either
x\in S
or
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
is unsatisfiable. In particular, since
S\subseteq\mathsf{nBot}_{\alpha}
, then
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
is unsatisfiable for all
x\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
. We will now establish that
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
by proving the following claims:

1.

\psi\Rightarrow\lnot\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}\Leftrightarrow\lnot\bigvee_{x\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}}\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Leftrightarrow\bigwedge_{x\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}}\lnot\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
. It will suffice to show that
\psi\Rightarrow\lnot\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
for all
x\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
. Take any
v\vDash\psi
. We know that
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
is unsatisfiable since
x\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
, therefore
v\vDash\lnot\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
, so we are done.

2.

\psi\Leftrightarrow\bigwedge_{x\in S}\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
. From
\psi\in\mathsf{valid}_{\alpha}(S)
, we immediately get that
\psi\Rightarrow\bigwedge_{x\in S}\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
. We now also show the reverse. Since
\psi\in\mathsf{valid}_{\alpha}(S)
, then
\psi\in\mathsf{forms}(\mathsf{tests}_{\alpha}(S))
. We now show that for every
y\in\mathsf{tests}_{\alpha}(S)
, either
\bigwedge_{x\in S}\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Rightarrow y
or
\bigwedge_{x\in S}\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Rightarrow\lnot y
. Take any
y\in\mathsf{tests}_{\alpha}(S)
. By the binary branching property,
\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(y)=\{z_{1},z_{2}\}
such that
\varphi_{\alpha}(z_{1})\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(y)\land y
and
\varphi_{\alpha}(z_{2})\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(y)\land\lnot y
. We also know that since
z_{1},z_{2}\in N_{\alpha}
, then for each
i\in\{1,2\}
either
z_{i}\in S
or
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(z_{i})
is unsatisfiable. We already know that
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(y)
since
y\in S
. We also know that
\psi\Rightarrow y
or
\psi\Rightarrow\lnot y
since
y\in\mathsf{tests}_{\alpha}(S)
and
\psi\in\mathsf{valid}_{\alpha}(S)
. Without loss of generality, suppose that
\psi\Rightarrow y
, then clearly
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(z_{1})\Leftrightarrow\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(y)\land y
is satisfiable, therefore
z_{1}\in S
. This clearly means that
\bigwedge_{x\in S}\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(z_{1})\Rightarrow y
. If instead we had that
\psi\Rightarrow\lnot y
, then we would have
\bigwedge_{x\in S}\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Rightarrow\lnot y
.

Therefore we have shown that
\bigwedge_{x\in S}\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
assigns a truth value to each
y\in\mathsf{vars}(\psi)
, and so
\bigwedge_{x\in S}\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Rightarrow\psi
.

3.

We now show that
S\subseteq\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha}
, that is
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\not\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
for all
x\in S
.
For the sake of contradiction, suppose that
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
for some
x\in S
. Since
\psi\in\mathsf{valid}_{\alpha}(S)
, we also have that
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
, which gives us:

\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}\Leftrightarrow\bigvee_{y\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}}\varphi_{\alpha}(y)

But this contradicts the fact that
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(y)
is unsatisfiable for all
y\in\mathsf{Bot}_{\alpha}
, therefore it cannot be true that
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
, and instead we have
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\not\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
.

4.

\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
is unsatisfiable for any
x\in\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha}\setminus S
. This is immediate, since
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
is unsatisfiable for any
x\notin S
.

So, we have now shown that
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
. Next, we show that
(\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta,\psi,S)=\min_{f(\psi)}
by showing the set inclusion in both directions.

Take any
x\in(\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta,\psi,S)
, so
x\in N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}
,
x\notin S
,
{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}\subseteq S
, and
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}(x)
. Clearly
x\notin N_{\alpha}
, otherwise one of
x\notin S
or
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}(x)=\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
would be violated. Now, take any
y<_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}x
, we know from
{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}\subseteq S
that
y\in S\subseteq N_{\alpha}
. So, since
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}(x)
, then
x\in N_{f(\psi)}
. Furthermore,
\{x\}=\min_{f(\psi)}
since we already established that all predecessors of
x
are in
\alpha
, and so
x
has no predecessors in
\beta_{f(\psi)}
.

We now show the reverse direction. Take any
\{x\}=\min_{f(\psi)}
. Clearly
x\in N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}
and
x\notin S
. By construction,
\varphi_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}(x)=\varphi_{f(\psi)}(x)\land\psi\Leftrightarrow\mathsf{true}\land\psi
(
\varphi_{f(\psi)}(x)\Leftrightarrow\mathsf{true}
since
x
is minimal and therefore no branches have occurred), so clearly
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}(x)
. Now take any
y<_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}x
, since
\{x\}=\min_{f(\psi)}
, then
y\in N_{\alpha}
, so
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(y)
. We already know that for all
y\in N_{\alpha}
either
y\in S
, or
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(y)
is unsatisfiable, but clearly the latter case is impossible, so
y\in S
. Therefore
{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}\subseteq S
, and we have shown all the conditions to guarantee that
x\in(\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta,\psi,S)
.
∎

Lemma 64
.

If
\alpha
is binary branching,
S\subseteq\mathsf{nBot}_{\alpha}
,
{}^{\ast}(\alpha,\psi,S)\neq\emptyset
, and
\psi\in\mathsf{valid}_{\alpha}(S)
, then
(\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta,\psi,S)=(\alpha,\psi,S)\neq\emptyset

Proof.

Since
{}^{\ast}(\alpha,\psi,S)\neq\emptyset
, then there exists
x\in^{\ast}(\alpha,\psi,S)
, meaning that
x\in N_{\alpha}\setminus S
and
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
is satisfiable. Take a minimal such
x
, so that there is no
y\in^{\ast}(\alpha,\psi,S)
such that
y<_{\alpha}x
.

We now show that
{x\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha}\subseteq S
. Take any
y<_{\alpha}x
. We know that
y\notin^{*}(\alpha,\psi,S)
, so either
y\in S
or
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(y)
is unsatisfiable. However, since
y<_{\alpha}x
, then
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(y)
, so
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(y)
must be satisfiable, meaning that the only option is that
y\in S
.

Now, since
\alpha
is binary branching, there are two options. The first option is that
x\in\mathsf{succ}_{\alpha}(y)
where
y
is a test node and
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(y)\land y
or
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(y)\land\lnot y
. Since
\psi\in\mathsf{valid}_{\alpha}(S)
and
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
is satisfiable, then it must be that
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
. If instead
x
is not the successor of any test nodes, then
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Leftrightarrow\bigwedge_{y\in\mathsf{pred}_{\alpha}(x)}\varphi_{\alpha}(y)
, so again since
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
is satisfiable, then it must be that
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
. In both cases, we have that
x\in(\alpha,\psi,S)
.

Finally, we show that
(\alpha,\psi,S)=(\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta,\psi,S)
by showing the set inclusion in both directions. Take any
y\in(\alpha,\psi,S)
, so
y\in N_{\alpha}\setminus S
such that
{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha}\subseteq S
and
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(y)
. Since
N_{\alpha}\subseteq_{\downarrow}N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}
, and
\varphi_{\alpha}(y)=\varphi_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}(y)
, this immediately gives us that
y\in N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}\setminus S
,
{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}\subseteq S
, and
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}(x)
therefore
y\in(\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta,\psi,S)
.

Now, for the reverse inclusion, take any
y\in(\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta,\psi,S)
, so
y\in N_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}\setminus S
such that
{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}\subseteq S
and
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}(x)
. From previously, we know that
x\in N_{\alpha}\setminus S
and
\psi\land\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
is satisfiable. Now, suppose for the sake of contradiction that
y\notin N_{\alpha}
, so it must be that
y\in N_{f(\psi^{\prime})}
for some
\psi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
. This implies that
x<_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}y
, as we will now show. First,
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}(y)=\varphi_{f(\psi^{\prime})}(y)\land\psi^{\prime}\Rightarrow\psi^{\prime}
, so
\psi^{\prime}\land\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
is satisfiable. If
x\notin\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha}
, then
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)\Rightarrow\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
, but we know that
\psi^{\prime}\Rightarrow\lnot\mathsf{stuck}_{\alpha}
, which contradicts that
\psi^{\prime}\land\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
is satisfiable. Therefore,
x\in\mathsf{ext}_{\alpha}
, and since
\psi^{\prime}
is maximal, then it must be the case that
\psi^{\prime}\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
already, which implies that
x<_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}y
. However, since
x\notin S
, then
{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}\not\subseteq S
, which is a contradiction. Therefore, it must be the case that
y\in N_{\alpha}
. We therefore have that
y\in N_{\alpha}\setminus S
,
{y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha}={y\mathclose{\downarrow}}_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}\subseteq S
, and
\psi\Rightarrow\varphi_{\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta}(y)=\varphi_{\alpha}(y)
; therefore,
y\in(\alpha,\psi,S)
.
∎

Lemma 65
.

For any binary branching
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathsk{lpof}}_{\mathsf{fin}}(L)
,
S\subseteq\mathsf{nBot}_{\alpha}
, and
\psi\in\mathsf{valid}_{\alpha}(S)
:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha\fatsemi\beta,\psi,S)=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\beta,\mathsf{true},\emptyset)^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha,\psi,S)

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the size of the set
{}^{\ast}(\alpha,\psi,S)
.

In the base case,
{}^{\ast}(\alpha,\psi,S)
is empty, so by
Lemma

63
, we know that
\psi\in\mathsf{br}_{\alpha}
and
(\alpha\fatsemi_{f}\beta,\psi,S)=\min_{f(\psi)}
. Since each lpo is single-rooted, then
\min_{f(\psi)}=\{x\}
for some
x\in N_{f(\psi)}
. This gives us:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha\fatsemi\beta,\psi,S)(s)

\displaystyle=\bignd_{x\in\mathsf{next}(\alpha\fatsemi\beta,\psi,S)}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha\fatsemi\beta,\psi,S\cup\{x\})^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket\lambda_{\alpha\fatsemi\beta}(x)\right\rrbracket{act}(s))&\text{if}~\lambda_{\alpha\fatsemi\beta}(x)\in\mathsk{act}\\
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsk{lpof}}(\alpha\fatsemi\beta,\psi\land\llparenthesis x=\left\llbracket\lambda_{\alpha\fatsemi\beta}(x)\right\rrbracket(s)\rrparenthesis,S\cup\{x\})(s)&\text{if}~\lambda_{\alpha\fatsemi\beta}(x)\in\mathsk{test}\\
